see <a href="#terminology-build-model">Build Model</a>.</p>
<p>The build platform is responsible for providing two things: <a href="#build-requirements-provenance-generation">provenance
generation</a> and <a href="#build-requirements-isolation-strength">isolation between builds</a>. The
<a href="#build-track-basics">Build level</a> describes the degree to which each of these
properties is met.</p>
<h4 id="build-requirements-provenance-generation">Provenance generation</h4>
<p>The build platform is responsible for generating provenance describing how the
package was produced.</p>
<p>The SLSA Build level describes the overall provenance integrity according to
minimum requirements on its:</p>
<ul>
<li><em>Completeness:</em> What information is contained in the provenance?</li>
<li><em>Authenticity:</em> How strongly can the provenance be tied back to the builder?</li>
<li><em>Accuracy:</em> How resistant is the provenance generation to tampering within
the build process?</li>
</ul>
<table>
<tr><th>Requirement<th>Description<th>L1<th>L2<th>L3
<tr id="provenance-exists"><td>Provenance Exists<td>
<p>The build process MUST generate provenance that unambiguously identifies the
output package by cryptographic digest and describes how that package was
produced. The format MUST be acceptable to the <a href="#verifying-artifacts-package-ecosystem">package ecosystem</a> and/or
<a href="#verifying-artifacts-consumer">consumer</a>.</p>
<p>It is RECOMMENDED to use the <a href="#provenance">SLSA Provenance</a> format and <a href="#attestation-model-recommended-suite">associated suite</a>
because it is designed to be interoperable, universal, and unambiguous when
used for SLSA. See that format’s documentation for requirements and
implementation guidelines.</p>
<p>If using an alternate format, it MUST contain the equivalent information as SLSA
Provenance at each level and SHOULD be bi-directionally translatable to SLSA
Provenance.</p>
<ul>
<li><em>Completeness:</em> Best effort. The provenance at L1 SHOULD contain sufficient
information to catch mistakes and simulate the user experience at higher
levels in the absence of tampering. In other words, the contents of the
provenance SHOULD be the same at all Build levels, but a few fields MAY be
absent at L1 if they are prohibitively expensive to implement.</li>
<li><em>Authenticity:</em> No requirements.</li>
<li><em>Accuracy:</em> No requirements.</li>
</ul>
<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="provenance-authentic"><td>Provenance is Authentic<td>
<p><em>Authenticity:</em> Consumers MUST be able to validate the authenticity of the
provenance attestation in order to:</p>
<ul>
<li><em>Ensure integrity:</em> Verify that the digital signature of the provenance
attestation is valid and the provenance was not tampered with after the
build.</li>
<li><em>Define trust:</em> Identify the build platform and other entities that are
necessary to trust in order to trust the artifact they produced.</li>
</ul>
<p>This SHOULD be through a digital signature from a private key accessible only
to the build platform component that generated the provenance attestation.</p>
<p>While many constraints affect choice of signing methodologies, it is
RECOMMENDED that build platforms use signing methodologies which improve the
ability to detect and remediate key compromise, such as methods which rely on
transparency logs or, when transparency isn’t appropriate, time stamping
services.</p>
<p>Authenticity allows the consumer to trust the contents of the provenance
attestation, such as the identity of the build platform.</p>
<p><em>Accuracy:</em> The provenance MUST be generated by the control plane (i.e. within
the trust boundary <a href="#provenance-model">identified in the provenance</a>) and not by a tenant of the
build platform (i.e. outside the trust boundary), except as noted below.</p>
<ul>
<li>The data in the provenance MUST be obtained from the build platform, either
because the generator <em>is</em> the build platform or because the provenance
generator reads the data directly from the build platform.</li>
<li>The build platform MUST have some security control to prevent tenants from
tampering with the provenance. However, there is no minimum bound on the
strength. The purpose is to deter adversaries who might face legal or
financial risk from evading controls.</li>
<li>Exceptions for fields that MAY be generated by a tenant of the build platform:
<ul>
<li>The names and cryptographic digests of the output artifacts, i.e.
<code>subject</code> in <a href="#provenance">SLSA Provenance</a>. See <a href="#threats-forged-digest">forge output digest of the
provenance</a> for explanation of why this is
acceptable.</li>
<li>Any field that is not marked as REQUIRED for Build L2. For example,
<code>resolvedDependencies</code> in <a href="#provenance">SLSA Provenance</a> MAY be tenant-generated at
Build L2. Builders SHOULD document any such cases of tenant-generated
fields.</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<p><em>Completeness:</em> SHOULD be complete.</p>
<ul>
<li>There MAY be <a href="#provenance-externalParameters">external parameters</a> that are not sufficiently captured in
the provenance.</li>
<li>Completeness of resolved dependencies is best effort.</li>
</ul>
<td> <td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="provenance-unforgeable"><td>Provenance is Unforgeable<td>
<p><em>Accuracy:</em> Provenance MUST be strongly resistant to forgery by tenants.</p>
<ul>
<li>Any secret material used for authenticating the provenance, for example the
signing key used to generate a digital signature, MUST be stored in a secure
management system appropriate for such material and accessible only to the
build service account.</li>
<li>Such secret material MUST NOT be accessible to the environment running
the user-defined build steps.</li>
<li>Every field in the provenance MUST be generated or verified by the build
platform in a trusted control plane. The user-controlled build steps MUST
NOT be able to inject or alter the contents, except as noted in <a href="#build-requirements-provenance-authentic">Provenance
is Authentic</a>. (Build L3 does not require additional
fields beyond those of L2.)</li>
</ul>
<p><em>Completeness:</em> SHOULD be complete.</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="#provenance-externalParameters">External parameters</a> MUST be fully enumerated.</li>
<li>Completeness of resolved dependencies is best effort.</li>
</ul>
<p>Note: This requirement was called “non-falsifiable” in the initial
<a href="/spec/v0.1/requirements#non-falsifiable">draft version (v0.1)</a>.</p>
<td> <td> <td>✓
</table>
<h4 id="build-requirements-isolation-strength">Isolation strength</h4>
<p>The build platform is responsible for isolating between builds, even within the
same tenant project. In other words, how strong of a guarantee do we have that
the build really executed correctly, without external influence?</p>
<p>The SLSA Build level describes the minimum bar for isolation strength. For more
information on assessing a build platform’s isolation strength, see
<a href="#assessing-build-platforms">Assessing build platforms</a>.</p>
<table>
<tr><th>Requirement<th>Description<th>L1<th>L2<th>L3
<tr id="hosted">
<td>Hosted
<td>
<p>All build steps ran using a hosted build platform on shared or dedicated
infrastructure, not on an individual’s workstation.</p>
<p>Examples: GitHub Actions, Google Cloud Build, Travis CI.</p>
<td> <td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="isolated">
<td>Isolated
<td>
<p>The build platform ensured that the build steps ran in an isolated environment,
free of unintended external influence. In other words, any external influence on
the build was specifically requested by the build itself. This MUST hold true
even between builds within the same tenant project.</p>
<p>The build platform MUST guarantee the following:</p>
<ul>
<li>It MUST NOT be possible for a build to access any secrets of the build
platform, such as the provenance signing key, because doing so would
compromise the authenticity of the provenance.</li>
<li>It MUST NOT be possible for two builds that overlap in time to influence one
another, such as by altering the memory of a different build process running
on the same machine.</li>
<li>It MUST NOT be possible for one build to persist or influence the build
environment of a subsequent build. In other words, an ephemeral build
environment MUST be provisioned for each build.</li>
<li>It MUST NOT be possible for one build to inject false entries into a build
cache used by another build, also known as “cache poisoning”. In other
words, the output of the build MUST be identical whether or not the cache is
used.</li>
<li>The build platform MUST NOT open services that allow for remote influence
unless all such interactions are captured as <code>externalParameters</code> in the
provenance.</li>
</ul>
<p>There are no sub-requirements on the build itself. Build L3 is limited to
ensuring that a well-intentioned build runs securely. It does not require that
a build platform prevents a producer from performing a risky or insecure build. In
particular, the “Isolated” requirement does not prohibit a build from calling
out to a remote execution service or a “self-hosted runner” that is outside the
trust boundary of the build platform.</p>
<p>NOTE: This requirement was split into “Isolated” and “Ephemeral Environment”
in the initial <a href="/spec/v0.1/requirements">draft version (v0.1)</a>.</p>
<p>NOTE: This requirement is not to be confused with “Hermetic”, which roughly
means that the build ran with no network access. Such a requirement requires
substantial changes to both the build platform and each individual build, and is
considered in the <a href="#future-directions">future directions</a>.</p>
<td> <td> <td>✓
</table>
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/build-requirements ends -->
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/distributing-provenance begins -->
   <h2 id="distributing-provenance">Build: Distributing provenance</h2>
<p>In order to make provenance for artifacts available after generation
for verification, SLSA requires the distribution and verification of provenance
metadata in the form of SLSA attestations.</p>
<p>This document provides specifications for distributing provenance and the
relationship between build artifacts and provenance (build attestations). It is
primarily concerned with artifacts for ecosystems that distribute build
artifacts, but some attention is also paid to ecosystems that distribute
container images or only distribute source artifacts, as many of the same
principles generally apply to any artifact or group of artifacts.</p>
<p>In addition, this document is primarily for the benefit of artifact
distributors, to understand how they can adopt the distribution of SLSA
provenance. It is primarily concerned with the means of distributing
attestations and the relationship of attestations to build artifacts, and not
with the specific format of the attestation itself.</p>
<p>The key words “MUST”, “MUST NOT”, “REQUIRED”, “SHALL”, “SHALL NOT”, “SHOULD”,
“SHOULD NOT”, “RECOMMENDED”, “MAY”, and “OPTIONAL” in this document are to be
interpreted as described in <a href="https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2119">RFC 2119</a>.</p>
<h3 id="distributing-provenance-background">Background</h3>
<p>The <a href="#verifying-artifacts-package-ecosystem">package ecosystem</a>’s maintainers are responsible for reliably
redistributing artifacts and provenance, making the producers’ expectations
available to consumers, and providing tools to enable safe artifact consumption
(e.g., whether an artifact meets its producer’s expectations).</p>
<h3 id="distributing-provenance-relationship-between-releases-and-attestations">Relationship between releases and attestations</h3>
<p>Attestations SHOULD be bound to artifacts, not releases.</p>
<p>A single “release” of a project, package, or library might include multiple
artifacts. These artifacts result from builds on different platforms,
architectures, or environments. The builds need not happen at roughly the same
point in time and might even span multiple days.</p>
<p>It is often difficult or impossible to determine when a release is ‘finished’
because many ecosystems allow adding new artifacts to old releases when adding
support for new platforms or architectures. Therefore, the set of attestations
for a given release MAY grow over time as additional builds and attestations
are created.</p>
<p>Thus, package ecosystems SHOULD support multiple individual attestations per
release. At the time of a given build, the relevant provenance for that build
can be added to the release, depending on the relationship to the given
artifacts.</p>
<h3 id="distributing-provenance-relationship-between-artifacts-and-attestations">Relationship between artifacts and attestations</h3>
<p>Package ecosystems SHOULD support a one-to-many relationship from build
artifacts to attestations to ensure that anyone is free to produce and publish
any attestation they might need. However, while there are lots of possible
attestations that can have a relationship to a given artifact, in this context
SLSA is primarily concerned with build attestations, i.e., provenance, and as
such, this specification only considers build attestations, produced by the
same maintainers as the artifacts themselves.</p>
<p>By providing provenance alongside an artifact in the manner specified by a
given ecosystem, maintainers are considered to be ‘elevating’ these build
attestations above all other possible attestations that could be provided by
third parties for a given artifact. The ultimate goal is for maintainers to
provide the provenance necessary for a repository to be able to verify some
potential policy that requires a certain SLSA level for publication, not
support the publication of arbitrary attestations by third parties.</p>
<p>As a result, this provenance SHOULD accompany the artifact at publish time, and
package ecosystems SHOULD provide a way to map a given artifact to its
corresponding attestations. The mappings can be either implicit (e.g., require a
custom filename schema that uniquely identifies the provenance over other
attestation types) or explicit (e.g., it could happen as a de-facto standard
based on where the attestation is published).</p>
<p>The provenance SHOULD have a filename that is directly related to the build
artifact filename. For example, for an artifact <code>&lt;filename&gt;.&lt;extension&gt;</code>, the
attestation is <code>&lt;filename&gt;.attestation</code> or some similar extension (for example
<a href="https://in-toto.io/">in-toto</a> recommends <code>&lt;filename&gt;.intoto.jsonl</code>).</p>
<h3 id="distributing-provenance-where-attestations-are-published">Where attestations are published</h3>
<p>There are a number of opportunities and venues to publish attestations during
and after the build process. Producers MUST publish attestations in at least
one place, and SHOULD publish attestations in more than one place:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Publish attestations alongside the source repository releases</strong>: If the
source repository hosting provider offers an artifact “release” feature,
such as <a href="https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/releasing-projects-on-github/about-releases">GitHub
releases</a>
or <a href="https://docs.gitlab.com/ee/user/project/releases/">GitLab releases</a>,
producers SHOULD include provenance as part of such releases. This option
has the benefit of requiring no changes to the package registry to support
provenance formats, but has the disadvantage of putting the source
repository hosting provider in the critical path for installers that want to
verify policy at build-time.</li>
<li><strong>Publish attestations alongside the artifact in the package registry</strong>:
Many software repositories already support some variety of publishing 1:1
related files alongside an artifact, sometimes known as “sidecar files”.
For example, PyPI supports publishing <code>.asc</code> files representing the PGP
signature for an artifact with the same filename (but different extension).
This option requires the mapping between artifact and attestation (or
attestation vessel) to be 1:1.</li>
<li><strong>Publish attestations elsewhere, record their existence in a transparency
log</strong>: Once an attestation has been generated and published for a build, a
hash of the attestation and a pointer to where it is indexed SHOULD be
published to a third-party transparency log that exists outside the source
repository and package registry. Not only are transparency logs such as
<a href="https://github.com/sigstore/rekor">Rekor from Sigstore</a> guaranteed
to be immutable, but they typically also make monitoring easier.
Requiring the presence of the attestation in a monitored transparency log
during verification helps ensure the attestation is trustworthy.</li>
</ul>
<p>Combining these options gives us a process for bootstrapping SLSA adoption
within an ecosystem, even if the package registry doesn’t support publishing
attestations. First, interested projects modify their release process to
produce SLSA provenance. Then, they publish that provenance to their source
repository. Finally, they publish the provenance to the package registry, if
and when the registry supports it.</p>
<p>Long-term, package registries SHOULD support uploading and distributing
provenance alongside the artifact. This model is preferred for two reasons:</p>
<ul>
<li>trust: clients already trust the package registry as the source of their
artifacts, and don’t need to trust an additional service;</li>
<li>reliability: clients already depend on the package registry as part of
their critical path, so distributing provenance via the registry avoids
adding an additional point of failure.</li>
</ul>
<p>Short term, consumers of build artifacts can bootstrap a manual policy by using
the source repository only for projects that publish all artifacts and
attestations to the source repository, and later extend this to all artifacts
published to the package registry via the canonical installation tools once
a given ecosystem supports them.</p>
<h3 id="distributing-provenance-immutability-of-attestations">Immutability of attestations</h3>
<p>Attestations SHOULD be immutable. Once an attestation is published as it
corresponds to a given artifact, that attestation is immutable and cannot be
overwritten later with a different attestation that refers to the same
artifact. Instead, a new release (and new artifacts) SHOULD be created.</p>
<h3 id="distributing-provenance-format-of-the-attestation">Format of the attestation</h3>
<p>The provenance is available to the consumer in a format that the consumer
accepts. The format SHOULD be in-toto
<a href="#build-provenance">SLSA Build Provenance</a>, but another format MAY be used if
both producer and consumer agree and it meets all the other requirements.</p>
<h3 id="distributing-provenance-considerations-for-source-based-ecosystems">Considerations for source-based ecosystems</h3>
<p>Some ecosystems have support for installing directly from source repositories
(an option for Python/<code>pip</code>, Go, etc.). In these cases, there is no need to
publish or verify provenance because there is no “build” step that translates
between a source repository and an artifact that is being installed.</p>
<p>However, for ecosystems that install from source repositories <em>via</em> some
intermediary (e.g., <a href="https://docs.brew.sh/Bottles">Homebrew installing from GitHub release artifacts generated
from the repository or GitHub Packages</a>, <a href="https://proxy.golang.org/">Go
installing through the Go module proxy</a>), these
ecosystems distribute “source archives” that are not the bit-for-bit identical
form from version control. These intermediaries are transforming the original
source repository in some way that constitutes a “build” and as a result SHOULD
be providing build provenance for this “package”, and the recommendations
outlined here apply.</p>
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/distributing-provenance ends -->
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/verifying-artifacts begins -->
   <h2 id="verifying-artifacts">Build: Verifying artifacts</h2>
<p>SLSA uses provenance to indicate whether an artifact is authentic or not, but
provenance doesn’t do anything unless somebody inspects it. SLSA calls that
inspection <strong>verification</strong>, and this section describes recommendations for how to
verify artifacts and their SLSA provenance.</p>
<p>This section is divided into several subsections. The first describes the process
for verifying an artifact and its provenance against a set of expectations. The
second describes how to form the expectations used to verify provenance. The
third discusses architecture choices for where provenance verification can
happen.</p>
<h3 id="verifying-artifacts-how-to-verify">How to verify</h3>
<p>Verification SHOULD include the following steps:</p>
<ul>
<li>Ensuring that the builder identity is one of those in the map of trusted
builder id’s to SLSA level.</li>
<li>Verifying the signature on the provenance envelope.</li>
<li>Ensuring that the values for <code>buildType</code> and <code>externalParameters</code> in the
provenance match the expected values. The package ecosystem MAY allow
an approved list of <code>externalParameters</code> to be ignored during verification.
Any unrecognized <code>externalParameters</code> SHOULD cause verification to fail.</li>
</ul>
<p><img src="/spec/v1.2/images/supply-chain-threats-build-verification.svg" alt="Threats covered by each step" /></p>
<p>See <a href="#terminology">Terminology</a> for an explanation of the supply chain model and
<a href="#threats">Threats &amp; mitigations</a> for a detailed explanation of each threat.</p>
<p><strong>Note:</strong> This subsection assumes that the provenance is in the recommended
<a href="/provenance/v1">provenance format</a>. If it is not, then the verifier SHOULD
perform equivalent checks on provenance fields that correspond to the ones
referenced here.</p>
<h4 id="verifying-artifacts-step-1-check-slsa-build-level">Step 1: Check SLSA Build level</h4>
<p>First, check the SLSA Build level by comparing the artifact to its provenance
and the provenance to a preconfigured root of trust. The goal is to ensure that
the provenance actually applies to the artifact in question and to assess the
trustworthiness of the provenance. This mitigates some or all of <a href="#threats">threats</a> “E”,
“F”, “G”, and “H”, depending on SLSA Build level and where verification happens.</p>
<p>Once, when bootstrapping the verifier:</p>
<ul>
<li>
<p>Configure the verifier’s roots of trust, meaning the recognized builder
identities and the maximum SLSA Build level each builder is trusted up to.
Different verifiers might use different roots of trust, but usually a
verifier uses the same roots of trust for all packages. This configuration
is likely in the form of a map from (builder public key identity,
<code>builder.id</code>) to (SLSA Build level).</p>
<details>
<summary>Example root of trust configuration</summary>
<p>The following snippet shows conceptually how a verifier’s roots of trust
might be configured using made-up syntax.</p>
<div class="language-jsonc highlighter-rouge"><div class="highlight"><pre class="highlight"><code><span class="nl">"slsaRootsOfTrust"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">[</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">// A builder trusted at SLSA Build L3, using a fixed public key.</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"publicKey"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"HKJEwI..."</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"builderId"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://somebuilder.example.com/slsa/l3"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"slsaBuildLevel"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="mi">3</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">},</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">// A different builder that claims to be SLSA Build L3,</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">// but this verifier only trusts it to L2.</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"publicKey"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"tLykq9..."</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"builderId"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://differentbuilder.example.com/slsa/l3"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"slsaBuildLevel"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="mi">2</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">},</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">// A builder that uses Sigstore for authentication.</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"sigstore"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
            </span><span class="nl">"root"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"global"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">  </span><span class="c1">// identifies fulcio/rekor roots</span><span class="w">
            </span><span class="nl">"subjectAlternativeNamePattern"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://github.com/slsa-framework/slsa-github-generator/.github/workflows/generator_generic_slsa3.yml@refs/tags/v*.*.*"</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="p">}</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"builderId"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://github.com/slsa-framework/slsa-github-generator/.github/workflows/generator_generic_slsa3.yml@refs/tags/v*.*.*"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"slsaBuildLevel"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="mi">3</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">}</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">...</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="p">]</span><span class="err">,</span><span class="w">
</span></code></pre></div></div>
</details>
</li>
</ul>
<p>Given an artifact and its provenance:</p>
<ol>
<li><a href="https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/docs/validation.md#validation-model">Verify</a> the envelope’s signature using the roots of
trust, resulting in a list of recognized public keys (or equivalent).</li>
<li><a href="https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/docs/validation.md#validation-model">Verify</a> that statement’s <code>subject</code> matches the digest of
the artifact in question.</li>
<li>Verify that the <code>predicateType</code> is <code>https://slsa.dev/provenance/v1</code>.</li>
<li>Look up the SLSA Build Level in the roots of trust, using the recognized
public keys and the <code>builder.id</code>, defaulting to SLSA Build L1.</li>
</ol>
<p>Resulting threat mitigation:</p>
<ul>
<li><a href="#threats-e-build-process">Threat “E”</a>: SLSA Build L3 requires protection against compromise of the
build process and provenance generation by an external adversary, such as
persistence between builds or theft of the provenance signing key. In other
words, SLSA Build L3 establishes that the provenance is accurate and
trustworthy, assuming you trust the build platform.
<ul>
<li>IMPORTANT: SLSA Build L3 does <strong>not</strong> cover compromise of the build
platform itself, such as by a malicious insider. Instead, verifiers
SHOULD carefully consider which build platforms are added to the roots
of trust. For advice on establishing trust in build platforms, see
<a href="#assessing-build-platforms">Assessing build platforms</a>.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><a href="#threats-f-artifact-publication">Threat “F”</a>: SLSA Build L2 covers tampering of the artifact or provenance
after the build. This is accomplished by verifying the <code>subject</code> and
signature in the steps above.</li>
<li><a href="#threats-g-distribution-channel">Threat “G”</a>: Verification by the consumer or otherwise outside of the
package registry covers compromise of the registry itself. (Verifying within
the registry at publication time is also valuable, but does not cover Threat
“G” or “I”.)</li>
<li><a href="#threats-i-usage">Threat “I”</a>: Verification by the consumer covers compromise of the package
in transit. (Many ecosystems also address this threat using package
signatures or checksums.)
<ul>
<li>NOTE: SLSA does not yet cover adversaries tricking a consumer to use an
unintended package, such as through typosquatting. Those threats are
discussed in more detail under <a href="#threats-h-package-selection">Threat “H”</a>.</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<h4 id="verifying-artifacts-step-2-check-expectations">Step 2: Check expectations</h4>
<p>Next, check that the package’s provenance meets your expectations for that
package in order to mitigate <a href="#threats-d-external-build-parameters">threat “D”</a>.</p>
<p>In our threat model, the adversary has the ability to invoke a build and to publish
to the registry. The adversary is not able to write to the source repository, nor do
they have insider access to any trusted systems. Your expectations SHOULD be
sufficient to detect or prevent this adversary from injecting unofficial
behavior into the package.</p>
<p>You SHOULD compare the provenance against expected values for at least the
following fields:</p>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>What</th>
<th>Why</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Builder identity from <a href="#verifying-artifacts-step-1-check-slsa-build-level">Step 1</a></td>
<td>To prevent an adversary from building the correct code on an unintended platform</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Canonical source repository</td>
<td>To prevent an adversary from building from an unofficial fork (or other disallowed source)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code>buildType</code></td>
<td>To ensure that <code>externalParameters</code> are interpreted as intended</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code>externalParameters</code></td>
<td>To prevent an adversary from injecting unofficial behavior</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p>Verification tools SHOULD reject unrecognized fields in <code>externalParameters</code> to
err on the side of caution. It is acceptable to allow a parameter to have a
range of values (possibly any value) if it is known that any value in the range
is safe. JSON comparison is sufficient for verifying parameters.</p>
<p>TIP: Difficulty in forming meaningful expectations about <code>externalParameters</code> can
be a sign that the <code>buildType</code>’s level of abstraction is too low. For example,
<code>externalParameters</code> that record a list of commands to run is likely impractical
to verify because the commands change on every build. Instead, consider a
<code>buildType</code> that defines the list of commands in a configuration file in a
source repository, then put only the source repository in
<code>externalParameters</code>. Such a design is easier to verify because the source
repository is constant across builds.</p>
<h4 id="verifying-artifacts-step-3-optional-check-dependencies-recursively">Step 3: (Optional) Check dependencies recursively</h4>
<p>Finally, recursively check the <code>resolvedDependencies</code> as available and to the
extent desired. Note that SLSA v1.0 does not have any requirements on the
completeness or verification of <code>resolvedDependencies</code>. However, one might wish
to verify dependencies in order to mitigate <a href="#threats-dependency-threats">dependency threats</a> and protect against
threats further up the supply chain. If <code>resolvedDependencies</code> is incomplete,
these checks can be done on a best-effort basis.</p>
<p>A <a href="/verification_summary">Verification Summary Attestation (VSA)</a> can make dependency verification
more efficient by recording the result of prior verifications. A trimming
heuristic or exception mechanism is almost always necessary when verifying
dependencies because there will be transitive dependencies that are SLSA Build
L0. (For example, consider the compiler’s compiler’s compiler’s … compiler.)</p>
<h3 id="verifying-artifacts-forming-expectations">Forming Expectations</h3>
<p><dfn>Expectations</dfn> are known provenance values that indicate the
corresponding artifact is authentic. For example, a package ecosystem may
maintain a mapping between package names and their canonical source
repositories. That mapping constitutes a set of expectations.</p>
<p>Possible models for forming expectations include:</p>
<ul>
<li>
<p><strong>Trust on first use:</strong> Accept the first version of the package as-is. On
each version update, compare the old provenance to the new provenance and
alert on any differences. This can be augmented by having rules about what
changes are benign, such as a parameter known to be safe or a heuristic
about safe git branches or tags.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Defined by producer:</strong> The package producer tells the verifier what their
expectations ought to be. In this model, the verifier SHOULD provide an
authenticated communication mechanism for the producer to set the package’s
expectations, and there SHOULD be some protection against an adversary
unilaterally modifying them. For example, modifications might require
two-party control, or consumers might have to accept each policy change
(another form of trust on first use).</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Defined in source:</strong> The source repository tells the verifier what their
expectations ought to be. In this model, the package name is immutably bound
to a source repository and all other external parameters are defined in the
source repository. This is how the Go ecosystem works, for example, since
the package name <em>is</em> the source repository location.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<p>It is important to note that expectations are tied to a <em>package name</em>, whereas
provenance is tied to an <em>artifact</em>. Different versions of the same package name
will likely have different artifacts and therefore different provenance. Similarly, an
artifact might have different names in different package ecosystems but use the same
provenance file.</p>
<h3 id="verifying-artifacts-architecture-options">Architecture options</h3>
<p>There are several options (non-mutually exclusive) for where provenance verification
can happen: the package ecosystem at upload time, the consumers at download time, or
via a continuous monitoring system. Each option comes with its own set of
considerations, but all are valid and at least one SHOULD be used.</p>
<p>More than one component can verify provenance. For example, even if a package
ecosystem verifies provenance, consumers who get artifacts from that package
ecosystem might wish to verify provenance themselves for defense in depth. They
can do so using either client-side verification tooling or by polling a
monitor.</p>
<!-- **TODO** Add a diagram. -->
<h4 id="verifying-artifacts-package-ecosystem">Package ecosystem</h4>
<p>A <dfn>package ecosystem</dfn> is a set of rules and conventions governing
how packages are distributed. Every package artifact has an ecosystem, whether it is
formal or ad-hoc. Some ecosystems are formal, such as language distribution
(e.g. <a href="https://www.pypa.io">Python/PyPA</a>), operating system distribution (e.g.
<a href="https://wiki.debian.org/DebianRepository/Format">Debian/Apt</a>), or artifact
distribution (e.g. <a href="https://github.com/opencontainers/distribution-spec">OCI</a>).
Other ecosystems are informal, such as a convention used within a company. Even
ad-hoc distribution of software, such as through a link on a website, is
considered an “ecosystem”. For more background, see
<a href="#terminology-package-model">Package Model</a>.</p>
<p>During package upload, a package ecosystem can ensure that the artifact’s
provenance matches the expected values for that package name’s provenance before
accepting it into the package registry.  This option is RECOMMENDED whenever
possible because doing so benefits all of the package ecosystem’s clients.</p>
<p>The package ecosystem is responsible for making its expectations available to
consumers, reliably redistributing artifacts and provenance, and providing tools
to enable safe artifact consumption (e.g. whether an artifact meets
expectations).</p>
<h4 id="verifying-artifacts-consumer">Consumer</h4>
<p>A package artifact’s <dfn>consumer</dfn> is the organization or individual that uses the
package artifact.</p>
<p>Consumers can form their own expectations for artifacts or use the default
expectations provided by the package producer and/or package ecosystem.
When forming their own expectations, the consumer uses client-side verification tooling to ensure
that the artifact’s provenance matches their expectations for that package
before use (e.g. during installation or deployment). Client-side verification
tooling can be either standalone, such as
<a href="https://github.com/slsa-framework/slsa-verifier">slsa-verifier</a>, or built into
the package ecosystem client.</p>
<h4 id="verifying-artifacts-monitor">Monitor</h4>
<p>A <dfn>monitor</dfn> is a service that verifies provenance for a set
of packages and publishes the result of that verification. The set of
packages verified by a monitor is arbitrary, though it MAY mimic the set
of packages published through one or more package ecosystems. The monitor
SHOULD publish its expectations for all the packages it verifies.</p>
<p>Consumers can continuously poll a monitor to detect artifacts that
do not meet the monitor’s expectations. Detecting artifacts that fail
verification is of limited benefit unless a human or automated system takes
action in response to the failed verification.</p>
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/verifying-artifacts ends -->
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/assessing-build-platforms begins -->
   <h2 id="assessing-build-platforms">Build: Assessing build platforms</h2>
<p>One of SLSA’s guiding <a href="#principles">principles</a> is to “trust platforms, verify
artifacts”. However, consumers cannot trust platforms to produce Build L3
artifacts and provenance unless they have some proof that the provenance is
<a href="#build-requirements-provenance-unforgeable">unforgeable</a> and the builds are
<a href="#build-requirements-isolated">isolated</a>.</p>
<p>This section describes the parts of a build platform that consumers SHOULD assess
and provides sample questions consumers can ask when assessing a build platform.
See also <a href="#threats">Threats &amp; mitigations</a> and the
<a href="#terminology-build-model">build model</a>.</p>
<h3 id="assessing-build-platforms-threats">Threats</h3>
<h4 id="assessing-build-platforms-adversary-goal">Adversary goal</h4>
<p>The SLSA Build track defends against an adversary whose primary goal is to
inject unofficial behavior into a package artifact while avoiding detection.
Remember that <a href="#verifying-artifacts">verifiers</a> only accept artifacts whose
provenance matches expectations. To bypass this, the adversary tries to either
(a) tamper with a legitimate build whose provenance already matches
expectations, or (b) tamper with an illegitimate build’s provenance to make it
match expectations.</p>
<p>More formally, if a build with external parameters P would produce an artifact
with binary hash X and a build with external parameters P’ would produce an
artifact with binary hash Y, they wish to produce provenance indicating a build
with external parameters P produced an artifact with binary hash Y.</p>
<p>See threats <a href="#threats-d-external-build-parameters">D</a>, <a href="#threats-e-build-process">E</a>, <a href="#threats-f-artifact-publication">F</a>, and <a href="#threats-g-distribution-channel">G</a> for examples of specific threats.</p>
<p>Note: Platform abuse (e.g. running non-build workloads) and attacks against
builder availability are out of scope of this document.</p>
<h4 id="assessing-build-platforms-adversary-profiles">Adversary profiles</h4>
<p>Consumers SHOULD also evaluate the build platform’s ability to defend against the
following types of adversaries.</p>
<ol>
<li>Project contributors, who can:
<ul>
<li>Create builds on the build platform. These are the adversary’s controlled
builds.</li>
<li>Modify one or more controlled builds’ external parameters.</li>
<li>Modify one or more controlled builds’ environments and run arbitrary
code inside those environments.</li>
<li>Read the target build’s source repo.</li>
<li>Fork the target build’s source repo.</li>
<li>Modify a fork of the target build’s source repo and build from it.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Project maintainer, who can:
<ul>
<li>Do everything listed under “project contributors”.</li>
<li>Create new builds under the target build’s project or identity.</li>
<li>Modify the target build’s source repo and build from it.</li>
<li>Modify the target build’s configuration.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Build platform administrators, who can:
<ul>
<li>Do everything listed under “project contributors” and “project
maintainers”.</li>
<li>Run arbitrary code on the build platform.</li>
<li>Read and modify network traffic.</li>
<li>Access the control plane’s cryptographic secrets.</li>
<li>Remotely access build environments (e.g. via SSH).</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ol>
<h3 id="assessing-build-platforms-build-platform-components">Build platform components</h3>
<p>Consumers SHOULD consider at least these five elements of the
<a href="#terminology-build-model">build model</a> when assessing build platforms for SLSA
conformance: external parameters, control plane, build environments, caches,
and outputs.</p>
<p><img src="/spec/v1.2/images/build-model.svg" alt="image" /></p>
<p>The following subsections detail these elements of the build model and give prompts
for assessing a build platform’s ability to produce SLSA Build L3 provenance.
The assessment SHOULD take into account the security model used to identify the
transitive closure of the <code>builder.id</code> for the
<a href="#build-provenance-model">provenance model</a>, specifically around the
platform’s boundaries, actors, and interfaces.</p>
<h4 id="assessing-build-platforms-external-parameters">External parameters</h4>
<p>External parameters are the external interface to the builder and include all
inputs to the build process. Examples include the source to be built, the build
definition/script to be executed, user-provided instructions to the
control plane for how to create the build environment (e.g. which operating
system to use), and any additional user-provided strings.</p>
<h5 id="assessing-build-platforms-prompts-for-assessing-external-parameters">Prompts for assessing external parameters</h5>
<ul>
<li>How does the control plane process user-provided external parameters?
Examples: sanitizing, parsing, not at all</li>
<li>Which external parameters are processed by the control plane and which are
processed by the build environment?</li>
<li>What sort of external parameters does the control plane accept for
build environment configuration?</li>
<li>How do you ensure that all external parameters are represented in the
provenance?</li>
<li>How will you ensure that future design changes will not add additional
external parameters without representing them in the provenance?</li>
</ul>
<h4 id="assessing-build-platforms-control-plane">Control plane</h4>
<p>The control plane is the build platform component that orchestrates each
independent build execution. It is responsible for setting up each build and
cleaning up afterwards. At SLSA Build L2+ the control plane generates and signs
provenance for each build performed on the build platform. The control plane is
operated by one or more administrators, who have privileges to modify the
control plane.</p>
<h5 id="assessing-build-platforms-prompts-for-assessing-the-control-plane">Prompts for assessing the control plane</h5>
<ul>
<li>
<p>Administration</p>
<ul>
<li>What are the ways an employee can use privileged access to influence a
build or provenance generation? Examples: physical access, terminal
access, access to cryptographic secrets</li>
<li>What controls are in place to detect or prevent the employee from
abusing such access? Examples: two-person approvals, audit logging,
workload identities</li>
<li>Roughly how many employees have such access?</li>
<li>How are privileged accounts protected? Examples: two-factor
authentication, client device security policies</li>
<li>What plans do you have for recovering from security incidents and platform
outages? Are they tested? How frequently?</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Provenance generation</p>
<ul>
<li>How does the control plane observe the build to ensure the provenance’s
accuracy?</li>
<li>Are there situations in which the control plane will not generate
provenance for a completed build? What are they?</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Development practices</p>
<ul>
<li>How do you track the control plane’s software and configuration?
Example: version control</li>
<li>How do you build confidence in the control plane’s software supply
chain? Example: SLSA L3+ provenance, build from source</li>
<li>How do you secure communications between builder components? Example:
TLS with certificate transparency.</li>
<li>Are you able to perform forensic analysis on compromised build
environments? How? Example: retain base images indefinitely</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Creating build environments</p>
<ul>
<li>How does the control plane share data with build environments? Example:
mounting a shared file system partition</li>
<li>How does the control plane protect its integrity from build
environments? Example: not mount its own file system partitions on
build environments</li>
<li>How does the control plane prevent build environments from accessing its
cryptographic secrets? Examples: dedicated secret storage, not mounting
its own file system partitions to build environments, hardware security
modules</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Managing cryptographic secrets</p>
<ul>
<li>How do you store the control plane’s cryptographic secrets?</li>
<li>Which parts of the organization have access to the control plane’s
cryptographic secrets?</li>
<li>What controls are in place to detect or prevent employees abusing such
access? Examples: two-person approvals, audit logging</li>
<li>How are secrets protected in memory? Examples: secrets are stored in
hardware security modules and backed up in secure cold storage</li>
<li>How frequently are cryptographic secrets rotated? Describe the rotation
process.</li>
<li>What is your plan for remediating cryptographic secret compromise? How
frequently is this plan tested?</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<h4 id="assessing-build-platforms-build-environment">Build environment</h4>
<p>The build environment is the independent execution context where the build
takes place. In the case of a distributed build, the build environment is the
collection of all execution contexts that run build steps. Each build
environment must be isolated from the control plane and from all other build
environments, including those running builds from the same tenant or project.
Tenants are free to modify the build environment arbitrarily. Build
environments must have a means to fetch input artifacts (source, dependencies,
etc).</p>
<h5 id="assessing-build-platforms-prompts-for-assessing-build-environments">Prompts for assessing build environments</h5>
<ul>
<li>
<p>Isolation technologies</p>
<ul>
<li>How are build environments isolated from the control plane and each
other? Examples: VMs, containers, sandboxed processes</li>
<li>How is separation achieved between trusted and untrusted processes?</li>
<li>How have you hardened your build environments against malicious tenants?
Examples: configuration hardening, limiting attack surface</li>
<li>How frequently do you update your isolation software?</li>
<li>What is your process for responding to vulnerability disclosures? What
about vulnerabilities in your dependencies?</li>
<li>What prevents a malicious build from gaining persistence and influencing
subsequent builds?</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Creation and destruction</p>
<ul>
<li>What operating system and utilities are available in build environments
on creation? How were these elements chosen? Examples: A minimal Linux
distribution with its package manager, OSX with HomeBrew</li>
<li>How long could a compromised build environment remain active in the
build platform?</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Network access</p>
<ul>
<li>Are build environments able to call out to remote execution? If so, how
do you prevent them from tampering with the control plane or other build
environments over the network?</li>
<li>Are build environments able to open services on the network? If so, how
do you prevent remote interference through these services?</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<h4 id="assessing-build-platforms-cache">Cache</h4>
<p>Builders may have zero or more caches to store frequently used dependencies.
Build environments may have either read-only or read-write access to caches.</p>
<h5 id="assessing-build-platforms-prompts-for-assessing-caches">Prompts for assessing caches</h5>
<ul>
<li>What sorts of caches are available to build environments?</li>
<li>How are those caches populated?</li>
<li>How are cache contents validated before use?</li>
</ul>
<h4 id="assessing-build-platforms-output-storage">Output storage</h4>
<p>Output Storage holds built artifacts and their provenance. Storage may either be
shared between build projects or allocated separately per-project.</p>
<h5 id="assessing-build-platforms-prompts-for-assessing-output-storage">Prompts for assessing output storage</h5>
<ul>
<li>How do you prevent builds from reading or overwriting files that belong to
another build? Example: authorization on storage</li>
<li>What processing, if any, does the control plane do on output artifacts?</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="assessing-build-platforms-builder-evaluation">Builder evaluation</h3>
<p>Organizations can either self-attest to their answers or seek certification from
a third-party auditor. Evidence for self-attestation should be published on
the internet and can include information such as the security model defined as
part of the provenance. Evidence submitted for third-party certification need not
be published.</p>
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/assessing-build-platforms ends -->
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/source-requirements begins -->
   <h2 id="source-requirements">Source: Requirements for producing source</h2>
<h3 id="source-requirements-objective">Objective</h3>
<p>The primary purpose of the SLSA Source track is to provide producers and consumers with increasing levels of trust in the source code they produce and consume.
It describes increasing levels of trustworthiness and completeness of how a source revision was created.</p>
<p>The expected process for creating a new revision is determined solely by that repository’s owner (the organization) who also determines the intent of the software in the repository and administers technical controls to enforce the process.</p>
<p>Consumers can review attestations to verify whether a particular revision meets their standards.</p>
<h3 id="source-requirements-definitions">Definitions</h3>
<p>A <strong>Version Control System (VCS)</strong> is a system of software and protocols for
managing the version history of a set of files. Git, Mercurial, and Subversion
are all examples of version control systems.</p>
<p>The following terms apply to Version Control Systems:</p>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Term</th>
<th>Description</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Source Repository (Repo)</td>
<td>A self-contained unit that holds the content and revision history for a set of files, along with related metadata like Branches and Tags.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Source Revision</td>
<td>A specific, logically immutable snapshot of the repository’s tracked files. It is uniquely identified by a revision identifier, such as a cryptographic hash like a Git commit SHA or a path-qualified sequential number like <code>25@trunk/</code> in SVN. A Source Revision includes both the content (the files) and its associated version control metadata, such as the author, timestamp, and parent revision(s). Note: Path qualification is needed for version control systems that represent Branches and Tags using paths, such as Subversion and Perforce.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Named Reference</td>
<td>A user-friendly name for a specific source revision, such as <code>main</code> or <code>v1.2.3</code>.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Change</td>
<td>A modification to the state of the Source Repository, such as creation of a new Source Revision based on a previous Source Revision, or creation, deletion, or modification of a Named Reference.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Change History</td>
<td>A record of the history of Source Revisions that preceded a specific revision.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Branch</td>
<td>A Named Reference that moves to track the Change History of a cohesive line of development within a Source Repository. E.g. <code>main</code>, <code>develop</code>, <code>feature-x</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><span id="tag">Tag</span></td>
<td>A Named Reference that is intended to be immutable. Once created, it is not moved to point to a different revision. E.g. <code>v1.2.3</code>, <code>release-20250722</code></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>NOTE:</strong> The ‘branch’ and ‘tag’ features within version control systems may
not always align with the ‘Branch’ and ‘Tag’ definitions provided in this
specification. For example, in git and other version control systems, the UX may
allow ‘tags’ to be moved. Patterns like <code>latest</code> and <code>nightly</code> tags rely on this.
For the purposes of this specification these would be classified as ‘Named References’ and not as ‘Tags’.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>A <strong>Source Control System (SCS)</strong> is a platform or combination of services
(self-hosted or SaaS) that hosts a Source Repository and provides a trusted
foundation for managing source revisions by enforcing policies for
authentication, authorization, and change management, such as mandatory code
reviews or passing status checks.</p>
<p>The following terms apply to Source Control Systems:</p>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Term</th>
<th>Description</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Organization</td>
<td>A set of people who collectively create Source Revisions within a Source Repository. Examples of organizations include open-source projects, a company, or a team within a company. The organization defines the goals of a Source Repository and the methods used to produce new Source Revisions.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Proposed Change</td>
<td>A proposal to make a Change in a Source Repository.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Source Provenance</td>
<td>Information about how a Source Revision came to exist, where it was hosted, when it was generated, what process was used, who the contributors were, and which parent revisions preceded it.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="source-requirements-source-roles">Source Roles</h4>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Role</th>
<th>Description</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td>Administrator</td>
<td>A human who can perform privileged operations on one or more projects. Privileged actions include, but are not limited to, modifying the change history and modifying project- or organization-wide security policies.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Trusted person</td>
<td>A human who is authorized by the organization to propose and approve changes to the source.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Trusted robot</td>
<td>Automation authorized by the organization to act in explicitly defined contexts. The robot’s identity and codebase cannot be unilaterally influenced.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Untrusted person</td>
<td>A human who has limited access to the project. They MAY be able to read the source. They MAY be able to propose or review changes to the source. They MAY NOT approve changes to the source or perform any privileged actions on the project.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h3 id="source-requirements-onboarding">Onboarding</h3>
<p>When onboarding a branch to the SLSA Source Track or increasing the level of
that branch, organizations are making claims about how the branch is managed
from that revision forward. This establishes <a href="#source-requirements-continuity">continuity</a>.</p>
<p>No claims are made for prior revisions.</p>
<h3 id="source-requirements-basics">Basics</h3>
<p>NOTE: This table presents a simplified view of the requirements. See the
<a href="#source-requirements-requirements">Requirements</a> subsection for the full list of requirements for each
level.</p>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Track/Level</th>
<th>Requirements</th>
<th>Focus</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><a href="#source-requirements-source-l1">Source L1</a></td>
<td>Use a version control system.</td>
<td>Generation of discrete Source Revisions for precise consumption.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><a href="#source-requirements-source-l2">Source L2</a></td>
<td>Preserve Change History and generate Source Provenance.</td>
<td>Reliable history through enforced controls and evidence.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><a href="#source-requirements-source-l3">Source L3</a></td>
<td>Enforce organizational technical controls.</td>
<td>Consumer knowledge of guaranteed technical controls.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><a href="#source-requirements-source-l4">Source L4</a></td>
<td>Require code review.</td>
<td>Improved code quality and resistance to insider threats.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<section id="source-requirements-source-l1">
<h4 id="source-requirements-level-1-version-controlled">Level 1: Version controlled</h4>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>The source is stored and managed through a modern version control system.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>Organizations currently storing source in non-standard ways who want to quickly gain some benefits of SLSA and better integrate with the SLSA ecosystem with minimal impact to their current workflows.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>Migrating to the appropriate tools is an important first step on the road to operational maturity.</p>
</dl>
</section>
<section id="source-requirements-source-l2">
<h4 id="source-requirements-level-2-history-amp-provenance">Level 2: History &amp; Provenance</h4>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>Branch history is continuous, immutable, and retained, and the
SCS issues Source Provenance Attestations for each new Source Revision.
The attestations provide contemporaneous, tamper-resistant evidence of when
changes were made, who made them, and which technical controls were enforced.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>All organizations of any size producing software of any kind.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>Reliable history allows organizations and consumers to track changes to software
over time, enabling attribution of those changes to the actors that made them.
Source Provenance provides strong, tamper-resistant evidence of the process that
was followed to produce a Source Revision.</p>
</dl>
</section>
<section id="source-requirements-source-l3">
<h4 id="source-requirements-level-3-continuous-technical-controls">Level 3: Continuous technical controls</h4>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>The SCS is configured to enforce the Organization’s technical controls for specific Named References within the Source Repository.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>Organizations that want to show evidence of their additional technical controls.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>A verifier can use this published data to ensure that a given Source Revision
was created in the correct way by verifying the Source Provenance or VSA.
Provides verifiers strong evidence of all technical controls enforced during the update of a Named Reference.</p>
</dl>
</section>
<section id="source-requirements-source-l4">
<h4 id="source-requirements-level-4-two-party-review">Level 4: Two-party review</h4>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>The SCS requires two trusted persons to review all changes to protected
branches.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>Organizations that want strong guarantees that the software they produce is not
subject to unilateral changes that would subvert their intent.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>Makes it harder for an actor to introduce malicious changes into the software
and makes it more likely that the source reflects the intent of the
organization.</p>
</dl>
</section>
<h3 id="source-requirements-requirements">Requirements</h3>
<p>Many examples in this document use the
<a href="https://git-scm.com/">git version control system</a>, but use of git is not a
requirement to meet any level on the SLSA source track.</p>
<h4 id="source-requirements-organization">Organization</h4>
<table>
<tr><th>Requirement<th>Description<th>L1<th>L2<th>L3<th>L4
<tr id="choose-scs"><td>Choose an appropriate Source Control System <a href="#source-requirements-choose-scs">🔗</a><td>
<p>An organization producing Source Revisions MUST select an SCS capable of reaching
their desired SLSA Source Level.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example, if an organization wishes to produce revisions at Source Level 3,
they MUST choose a Source Control System capable of producing Source Level 3
attestations.</p>
</blockquote>
<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="access-and-history"><td>Configure the SCS to control access and enforce history <a href="#source-requirements-access-and-history">🔗</a><td>
<p>The organization MUST configure access controls to restrict sensitive operations
on the Source Repository. These controls MUST be implemented using the
SCS-provided <a href="#source-requirements-identity-management">Identity Management capability</a>.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example, an organization may configure the SCS to assign users to a
<code>maintainers</code> role and only allow users in <code>maintainers</code> to make updates to
<code>main</code>.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The SCS MUST be configured to produce a reliable <a href="#source-requirements-history">Change History</a> for
its consumable Source Revisions.
If the SCS provides this capability by design, no additional controls are needed.
Otherwise the organization MUST provide evidence of <a href="#source-requirements-continuity">continuous enforcement</a>.</p>
<p>If the SCS supports <a href="#source-requirements-tag">Tags</a>, the SCS MUST be configured to prevent them
from being moved or deleted.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example, if a git tag <code>release1</code> is used to indicate a release revision
with ID <code>abc123</code>, controls must be configured to prevent that tag from being
updated to any other revision in the future.
Evidence of these controls (and their continuity) will appear in the Source
Provenance documents for revision <code>abc123</code>.</p>
</blockquote>
<td><td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="safe-expunging-process"><td>Safe Expunging Process <a href="#source-requirements-safe-expunging-process">🔗</a><td>
<p>SCSs MAY allow the organization to expunge (remove) content from a repository
and its change history without leaving a public record of the removed content,
but the organization MUST only allow these changes in order to meet legal or
privacy compliance requirements. Content changed under this process includes
changing files, history, references, or any other metadata stored by the SCS.</p>
<h5 id="source-requirements-warning">Warning</h5>
<p>Removing a revision from a repository is similar to deleting a package version
from a registry: it’s almost impossible to estimate the amount of downstream
supply chain impact.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example, in Git, each revision ID is based on the ones before it.
When you remove a revision, you must generate new revisions (and new revision IDs)
for any revisions that were built on top of it. Consumers who took a dependency
on the old revisions may now be unable to refer to the revision they’ve already
integrated into their products.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>It may be the case that the specific set of changes targeted by a legal takedown
can be expunged in ways that do not impact consumed revisions, which can mitigate
these problems.</p>
<p>It is also the case that removing content from a repository won’t necessarily
remove it everywhere.
The content may still exist in other copies of the repository, either in backups
or on developer machines.</p>
<h5 id="source-requirements-process">Process</h5>
<p>An organization MUST document the Safe Expunging Process and describe how
requests and actions are tracked and SHOULD log the fact that content was
removed. Different organizations and tech stacks may have different approaches
to the problem.</p>
<p>SCSs SHOULD have technical mechanisms in place which require an Administrator
plus at least one additional ‘trusted person’ to trigger any expunging
(removals) made under this process.</p>
<p>The application of the Safe Expunging Process and the resulting logs MAY be
private to prevent calling attention to potentially sensitive data or to comply
with local laws and regulations. Organizations SHOULD prefer to make logs public
if possible.</p>
<td><td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="technical-controls"><td>Continuous technical controls <a href="#source-requirements-technical-controls">🔗</a><td>
<p>The organization MUST provide evidence of continuous enforcement via technical
controls for any claims made in the Source Provenance attestations or VSAs (see
<a href="#source-requirements-continuity">control continuity</a>).</p>
<p>The organization MUST document the meaning of their enforced technical controls.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example, if an organization implements a technical control via a custom
tool (such as required GitHub Actions workflow), it must indicate the name of
this tool, what it accomplishes, and how to find its evidence in the provenance
attestation.</p>
</blockquote>
<blockquote>
<p>For another example, if the organization claims that all consumable Source
Revisions on the <code>main</code> branch were tested prior to acceptance, this MUST be
explicitly enforced in the SCS.</p>
</blockquote>
<td><td><td>✓<td>✓
</table>
<h4 id="source-requirements-source-control-system">Source Control System</h4>
<table>
<tr><th>Requirement<th>Description<th>L1<th>L2<th>L3<th>L4
<tr id="repository-ids"><td>Repositories are uniquely identifiable <a href="#source-requirements-repository-ids">🔗</a><td>
<p>The repository ID is defined by the SCS and MUST be uniquely identifiable within
the context of the SCS with a stable locator, such as a URI.</p>
<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="revision-ids"><td>Revisions are immutable and uniquely identifiable <a href="#source-requirements-revision-ids">🔗</a><td>
The revision ID is defined by the SCS and MUST be uniquely identifiable within the context of the repository.
When the revision ID is a digest of the content of the revision (as in git) nothing more is needed.
When the revision ID is a number or otherwise not a digest, then the SCS MUST document how the immutability of the revision is established.
The same revision ID MAY be present in multiple repositories.
<p>See also <a href="https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/spec/v1/digest_set.md#use-cases-for-non-cryptographic-immutable-digests">Use cases for non-cryptographic, immutable, digests</a>.</p>
<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="human-readable-diff"><td>Human readable changes <a href="#source-requirements-human-readable-diff">🔗</a><td>
<p>The SCS MUST provide tooling to display Changes between one Source Revision and
another in a human readable form (e.g. ‘diffs’) for all plain-text changes and
SHOULD provide mechanisms to provide human understandable interpretations of
non-plain-text changes (e.g. render images, verify and display provenance for
binaries, etc.).</p>
<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="source-summary"><td>Source Verification Summary Attestations <a href="#source-requirements-source-summary">🔗</a><td>
<p>The SCS MUST generate a
<a href="#source-requirements-source-verification-summary-attestation">source verification summary attestation</a> (Source VSA)
to indicate the SLSA Source Level of any revision at Level 1 or above.</p>
<p>If a consumer is authorized to access a revision, they MUST be able to fetch the
corresponding Source VSA.</p>
<p>If the SCS DOES NOT generate a VSA for a revision, the revision has Source Level
0.</p>
<p>At Source Levels 1 and 2 the SCS MAY issue these attestations based on its
understanding of the underlying system (e.g. based on design docs, security
reviews, etc.), but at Level 2+ the SCS MUST use the SCS-issued
<a href="#source-requirements-source-provenance">source provenance</a> when issuing the VSAs.</p>
<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="history"><td>History <a href="#source-requirements-history">🔗</a><td>
<p>There are three key aspects to change history:</p>
<ol>
<li>What were all the previous states of a Branch?</li>
<li>How and when did they change?</li>
<li>How does the current revision relate to previous revisions?</li>
</ol>
<p>To answer these questions, the SCS MUST record all changes to Named References,
including when they occurred, who made them, and the new Source Revision ID.</p>
<p>If Source Revisions have ancestry relationships in the VCS, the SCS MUST ensure
that a Branch can only be updated to point to revisions that descend from the
current revision.
In git, this requires a technical control to prohibit <code>git push --force</code>.</p>
<p>This requirement captures evidence that the organization intended to make the
changes captured by the new revision.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example, if a branch currently points to revision <code>a</code>, it may only be
moved to a new revision <code>b</code> if <code>a</code> is an ancestor of <code>b</code>.</p>
</blockquote>
<td><td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="continuity"><td>Continuity <a href="#source-requirements-continuity">🔗</a><td>
<p>Technical Controls are only effective if they are used continuously in the
history of a Branch.
‘Control continuity’ reflects an organization’s ongoing commitment to a
technical control.</p>
<p>For each technical control claimed in a VSA, continuity MUST be established and
tracked from a specific start revision.
If there is a lapse in continuity for a specific control, continuity of that
control MUST be re-established from a new revision.</p>
<p>Exceptions to the continuity requirement are allowed via the <a href="#source-requirements-safe-expunging-process">safe expunging process</a>.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example, the <code>main</code> branch currently points to revision <code>a</code> when a new
technical control <code>t</code> is configured.
The next revision on the <code>main</code> branch, <code>b</code> will be the first revision that was
protected by <code>t</code> and <code>b</code> is the first revision in the “continuity” of <code>t</code>.
Any revisions added to <code>main</code> while <code>t</code> is disabled will reset the continuity of <code>t</code>.</p>
</blockquote>
<td><td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="identity-management"><td>Identity Management <a href="#source-requirements-identity-management">🔗</a><td>
<p>The SCS MUST provide an identity management system or some other means of
identifying and authenticating actors.</p>
<p>The SCS MUST allow organizations to specify which actors and roles are allowed
to perform sensitive actions within a repository (e.g. creation or updates of
branches, approval of changes).</p>
<p>Depending on the SCS, identity management may be provided by source control
services (e.g., GitHub, GitLab), implemented using cryptographic signatures
(e.g., using gittuf to manage public keys for actors), or extending existing
authentication systems used by the organization (e.g., Active Directory, Okta,
etc.).</p>
<p>The SCS MUST document how actors are identified for the purposes of attribution.</p>
<p>Activities conducted on the SCS SHOULD be attributed to authenticated
identities.</p>
<td><td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="source-provenance"><td>Source Provenance <a href="#source-requirements-source-provenance">🔗</a><td>
<p><a href="#source-requirements-source-provenance-attestations">Source Provenance</a> are attestations that
contain information about how a specific revision was created and how it came to
exist on a protected branch or how a tag came to point at it. They are
associated with the revision identifier delivered to consumers and are a
statement of fact from the perspective of the SCS. The SCS MUST document the
format and intent of all Source Provenance attestations it produces.</p>
<p>Source Provenance MUST be created contemporaneously with the branch being
updated such that they provide a credible, auditable, record of changes.</p>
<p>If a consumer is authorized to access a revision, they MUST be able to access the
corresponding Source Provenance documents for that revision.</p>
<p>It is possible that an SCS can make no claims about a particular revision.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example, this would happen if the revision was created on another SCS,
on an unprotected branch (such as a <code>topic</code> branch), or if the revision was not
the result of the expected process.</p>
</blockquote>
<td><td>✓<td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="protected-refs"><td>Protected Named References <a href="#source-requirements-protected-refs">🔗</a><td>
<p>The SCS MUST provide the ability for an organization to enforce customized technical controls for Named References.</p>
<p>The SCS MUST provide a mechanism for organizations to indicate which Named
References should be protected by technical controls.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example, the organization may instruct the SCS to protect <code>main</code> and
<code>refs/heads/releases/*</code>, but not <code>refs/heads/experimental/*</code> using branch
protection rules (e.g. <a href="https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/about-rulesets">GitHub</a>,
<a href="https://docs.gitlab.com/ee/user/project/repository/branches/protected.html">GitLab</a>)
or via the application and verification of <a href="https://github.com/gittuf/gittuf">gittuf</a>
policies.</p>
</blockquote>
<blockquote>
<p>For another example, the organization may instruct the SCS to prevent the deletion of
all <code>refs/tags/releases/*</code> using tag protection rules
(e.g. <a href="https://docs.github.com/en/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-rulesets/about-rulesets">GitHub</a>,
<a href="https://docs.gitlab.com/user/project/protected_tags/">GitLab</a>)
or via the application and verification of <a href="https://github.com/gittuf/gittuf">gittuf</a>
policies.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The SCS MUST</p>
<ul>
<li>Record technical controls enforced on Named References in contemporaneously
produced attestations associated with the corresponding Source Revisions.</li>
<li>Allow organizations to provide
<a href="#source-requirements-additional-properties">organization-specified properties</a> to be included in the
<a href="#source-requirements-source-verification-summary-attestation">Source VSA</a> when the corresponding controls are
enforced.</li>
<li>Allow organizations to distribute additional attestations related to their
technical controls to consumers authorized to access the corresponding Source
Revision.</li>
<li>Prevent organization-specified properties from beginning with any value
other than <code>ORG_SOURCE_</code>.</li>
</ul>
<td><td><td>✓<td>✓
<tr id="two-party-review"><td>Two-party review <a href="#source-requirements-two-party-review">🔗</a><td>
<p>Changes in protected branches MUST be agreed to by two or more trusted persons prior to submission.
The following combinations are acceptable:</p>
<ul>
<li>Uploader and reviewer are two different trusted persons.</li>
<li>Two different reviewers are trusted persons.</li>
</ul>
<p>Reviews SHOULD cover, at least, security relevant properties of the code.</p>
<p><strong>[Final revision approved]</strong> This requirement applies to the final revision
submitted. I.e., if additional changes are made during the review process, those changes MUST
be reviewed as well.</p>
<p><strong>[Context-specific approvals]</strong> Approvals are for a specific context, such as a
repo + branch in git. Moving fully reviewed content from one context to another
still requires review. The exact definition of “context” depends on the project,
and this does not preclude well-understood automatic merges, such as cutting a release branch.</p>
<p><strong>[Informed Review]</strong> The SCS MUST present reviewers with a clear representation of the result of accepting the proposed change. See <a href="#source-requirements-human-readable-diff">Human Readable Changes</a>.</p>
<p><strong>[Trusted Robot Contributions]</strong> An organization MAY choose to grant a Trusted
Robot a perpetual exception to a policy (e.g. a bot may be able to merge a change
that has not been reviewed by two parties).</p>
<p>Examples:</p>
<ul>
<li>Import and migration bots that move code from one repo to another.</li>
<li>Dependabot</li>
</ul>
<td><td><td><td>✓
</table>
<h3 id="source-requirements-communicating-source-levels">Communicating source levels</h3>
<p>SLSA source level details are communicated using attestations.
These attestations either refer to a source revision itself or provide context needed to evaluate an attestation that <em>does</em> refer to a revision.</p>
<p>There are two broad categories of source attestations within the source track:</p>
<ol>
<li>Source verification summary attestations (Source VSAs): Used to communicate to downstream users what high level security properties a given source revision meets.</li>
<li>Source provenance attestations: Provide trustworthy, tamper-proof, metadata with the necessary information to determine what high level security properties a given source revision has.</li>
</ol>
<p>To provide interoperability and ensure ease of use, it’s essential that the Source VSAs are applicable across all Source Control Systems.
However, due to the significant differences in how SCSs operate and how they may choose to meet the Source Track requirements, it is preferable to
allow for flexibility with the full source provenance attestations. To that end, SLSA leaves source provenance attestations undefined and up to the SCSs to determine
what works best in their environment.</p>
<h4 id="source-requirements-source-verification-summary-attestation">Source verification summary attestation</h4>
<p>Source verification summary attestations (Source VSAs) are issued by some authority that has sufficient evidence to make the determination of a given
revision’s source level.  Source VSAs convey properties about the revision as a whole and summarize properties computed over all
the changes that contributed to that revision over its history.</p>
<p>The source track issues Source VSAs using the <a href="#verification_summary">Verification Summary Attestations</a> format as follows:</p>
<ol>
<li><code>subject.uri</code> SHOULD be set to a URI where a human can find details about
the revision. This field is not intended for policy decisions. Instead, it
is only intended to direct a human investigating verification failures.
<ul>
<li>For example: <code>https://github.com/slsa-framework/slsa/commit/6ff3cd75c8c9e0fcedc62bd6a79cf006f185cedb</code></li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><code>subject.digest</code> MUST include the revision identifier (e.g. <code>gitCommit</code>) and MAY include other digests over the contents of the revision (e.g. <code>gitTree</code>, <code>dirHash</code>, etc.).
