SCSs that do not use cryptographic digests MUST define a canonical type that is used to identify immutable revisions and MUST include the repository within the type<sup class="footnote-ref"><a href="#source-requirements-fn1" id="fnref1">1</a></sup>.
<ul>
<li>For example: <code>svn_revision_id: svn+https://svn.myproject.org/svn/MyProject/trunk@2019</code></li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><code>subject.annotations.sourceRefs</code> SHOULD be set to a list of references that pointed to this revision when the attestation was created. The list MAY be non-exhaustive.
<ul>
<li>git references MUST be fully qualified (e.g. <code>refs/heads/main</code> or <code>refs/tags/v1.0</code>) to reduce the likelihood of confusing downstream tooling.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li><code>resourceUri</code> MUST be set to the URI of the repository, preferably using <a href="https://spdx.github.io/spdx-spec/v2.3/package-information/#77-package-download-location-field">SPDX Download Location</a>.
E.g. <code>git+https://github.com/foo/hello-world</code>.</li>
<li><code>verifiedLevels</code> MUST include the SLSA source track level the SCS asserts the revision meets. One of <code>SLSA_SOURCE_LEVEL_0</code>, <code>SLSA_SOURCE_LEVEL_1</code>, <code>SLSA_SOURCE_LEVEL_2</code>, <code>SLSA_SOURCE_LEVEL_3</code>.
MAY include additional properties as asserted by the SCS.  The SCS MUST include <em>only</em> the highest SLSA source level met by the revision.</li>
<li><code>dependencyLevels</code> MAY be empty as source revisions are typically terminal nodes in a supply chain. For example, this could be used to indicate the source level of any git submodules present in the revision.</li>
</ol>
<h5 id="source-requirements-additional-properties">Additional properties</h5>
<p>The SLSA source track MAY create additional properties to include in
<code>verifiedLevels</code> which attest to other claims concerning a revision.</p>
<p>The SCS MAY embed additional properties within <code>verifiedLevels</code> provided by the
organization as long as they are prefixed by <code>ORG_SOURCE_</code>  to distinguish them
from other properties the SCS may wish to use. The SCS MUST enforce the use of
this prefix for such properties. An organization MAY further differentiate
properties using:</p>
<ul>
<li><code>ORG_SOURCE_</code> to indicate a property that is meant for consumption by
external consumers.</li>
<li><code>ORG_SOURCE_INTERNAL_</code> to indicate a property that is not meant for
consumption by external consumers.</li>
</ul>
<p>The meaning of the properties is left entirely to the organization.</p>
<h5 id="source-requirements-populating-sourcerefs">Populating sourceRefs</h5>
<p>The Source VSA issuer may choose to populate <code>sourceRefs</code> in any way they wish.
Downstream users are expected to be familiar with the method used by the issuer.</p>
<p>Example implementations:</p>
<ul>
<li>Issue a new VSA for each merged Pull Request and add the destination branch to <code>sourceRefs</code>.</li>
<li>Issue a new VSA each time a Named Reference is updated to point to a new revision.</li>
</ul>
<h5 id="source-requirements-example">Example</h5>
<div class="language-json highlighter-rouge"><div class="highlight"><pre class="highlight"><code><span class="nl">"_type"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://in-toto.io/Statement/v1"</span><span class="err">,</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">"subject"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">[{</span><span class="w">
  </span><span class="nl">"uri"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://github.com/foo/hello-world/commit/9a04d1ee393b5be2773b1ce204f61fe0fd02366a"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
  </span><span class="nl">"digest"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="nl">"gitCommit"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"9a04d1ee393b5be2773b1ce204f61fe0fd02366a"</span><span class="p">},</span><span class="w">
  </span><span class="nl">"annotations"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="nl">"sourceRefs"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">"refs/heads/main"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"refs/heads/release_1.0"</span><span class="p">]}</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="p">}]</span><span class="err">,</span><span class="w">
<p></span><span class="nl">“predicateType”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">“<a href="https://slsa.dev/verification_summary/v1">https://slsa.dev/verification_summary/v1</a>”</span><span class="err">,</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">“predicate”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">“verifier”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">“id”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">“<a href="https://example.com/source_verifier">https://example.com/source_verifier</a>”</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="p">},</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">“timeVerified”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">“1985-04-12T23:20:50.52Z”</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">“resourceUri”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">“git+<a href="https://github.com/foo/hello-world">https://github.com/foo/hello-world</a>”</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">“policy”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">“uri”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">“<a href="https://example.com/slsa_source.policy">https://example.com/slsa_source.policy</a>”</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="p">},</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">“verificationResult”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">“PASSED”</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="nl">“verifiedLevels”</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">[</span><span class="s2">“SLSA_SOURCE_LEVEL_3”</span><span class="p">],</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="p">}</span><span class="w">
</span></code></pre></div></div></p>
<h5 id="source-requirements-how-to-verify">How to verify</h5>
<p>See <a href="#verifying-source">Verifying Source</a> for instructions how to verify
VSAs for Source Revisions.</p>
<h4 id="source-requirements-source-provenance-attestations">Source provenance attestations</h4>
<p>Source provenance attestations provide tamper-proof evidence (<a href="#attestation-model">attestation model</a>)
that can be used to determine what SLSA Source Level or other high-level properties a given revision meets.
This evidence can be used by:</p>
<ul>
<li>an authority as the basis for issuing a <a href="#source-requirements-source-verification-summary-attestation">Source VSA</a></li>
<li>a consumer to cross-check a <a href="#source-requirements-source-verification-summary-attestation">Source VSA</a> they received for a revision</li>
<li>a consumer to enforce a more detailed policy than the organization’s own process</li>
</ul>
<p>SCSs may have different methods of operating that necessitate different forms of evidence.
E.g. GitHub-based workflows may need different evidence than Gerrit-based workflows, which would both likely be different from workflows that
operate over Subversion repositories.</p>
<p>These differences also mean that, depending on the configuration, the issuers of provenance attestations may vary from implementation to implementation, often because entities with the knowledge to issue them may vary.
The authority that issues <a href="#source-requirements-source-verification-summary-attestation">Source VSAs</a> MUST understand which entity should issue each provenance attestation type, and ensure all source provenance attestations come from their expected issuers.</p>
<p>‘Source provenance attestations’ is a generic term used to refer to any type of attestation that provides evidence of the process used to create a revision.</p>
<p>Example source provenance attestations:</p>
<ul>
<li>A TBD attestation which describes the revision’s parents and the actors involved in creating this revision.</li>
<li>A “code review” attestation which describes the basics of any code review that took place.</li>
<li>An “authentication” attestation which describes how the actors involved in any revision were authenticated.</li>
<li>A <a href="https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/spec/predicates/vuln.md">Vuln Scan attestation</a>
which describes the results of a vulnerability scan over the contents of the revision.</li>
<li>A <a href="https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/spec/predicates/test-result.md">Test Results attestation</a>
which describes the results of any tests run on the revision.</li>
<li>An <a href="https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/spec/predicates/spdx.md">SPDX attestation</a>
which provides a software bill of materials for the revision.</li>
<li>A <a href="https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/spec/predicates/scai.md">SCAI attestation</a> used to
describe which source quality tools were run on the revision.</li>
</ul>
<p>Irrespective of the types of provenance attestations generated by an SCS and
their implementations, the SCS MUST document provenance
formats, and how each provenance attestation can be used to reason about the
revision’s properties recorded in the summary attestation.</p>
<h3 id="source-requirements-future-considerations">Future Considerations</h3>
<h4 id="source-requirements-authentication">Authentication</h4>
<ul>
<li>Better protection against phishing by forbidding second factors that are not
phishing resistant.</li>
<li>Protect against authentication token theft by forbidding bearer tokens
(e.g. PATs).</li>
<li>Including length of continuity in the VSAs</li>
</ul>
<section class="footnotes">
<ol>
<li id="fn1">
<p>in-toto attestations allow non-cryptographic digest types: <a href="https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/spec/v1/digest_set.md#supported-algorithms">https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/spec/v1/digest_set.md#supported-algorithms</a>. <a href="#source-requirements-fnref1" class="footnote-backref">↩</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
</section>
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/source-requirements ends -->
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/verifying-source begins -->
   <h2 id="verifying-source">Source: Verifying source</h2>
<p>SLSA uses attestations to indicate security claims associated with a repository
revision, but attestations don’t do anything unless somebody inspects them. SLSA
calls that inspection <strong>verification</strong>, and this section describes how to verify
properties of source revisions using
<a href="#source-requirements-communicating-source-levels">the attestations</a> associated
with those revisions.</p>
<p>At Source L3+, Source Control Systems (SCSs) issue detailed
<a href="#source-requirements-source-provenance-attestations">provenance attestations</a> of
the process that was used to create specific revisions of a repository. These
provenance attestations are issued in bespoke formats and may be too burdensome
to use in some use cases.</p>
<p><a href="#source-requirements-source-verification-summary-attestation">Source Verification Summary Attestations</a>
(Source VSAs) address this, making verification more efficient and ergonomic by
recording the result of prior verifications. Source VSAs may be issued by a VSA
provider to make a SLSA source level determination based on the content of those
attestations.</p>
<h3 id="verifying-source-how-to-verify-a-source-revision">How to verify a source revision</h3>
<p>The source consumer checks:</p>
<ol>
<li>If they trust the SCS that issued the VSA and if the VSA applies to the
revision they’ve fetched.</li>
<li>If the claims made in the VSA match their expectations for how the source
should be managed.</li>
<li>(Optional): If the evidence presented in the source provenance matches the
claims made in the VSA.</li>
</ol>
<h4 id="verifying-source-step-1-check-the-scs">Step 1: Check the SCS</h4>
<p>First, check the SLSA Source level by comparing the artifact to its VSA and the
VSA to a preconfigured root of trust. The goal is to ensure that the VSA
actually applies to the artifact in question and to assess the trustworthiness
of the VSA. This mitigates threats within “B” and “C”, depending on SLSA Source
level.</p>
<p>Once, when bootstrapping the verifier:</p>
<ul>
<li>
<p>Configure the verifier’s roots of trust, meaning the recognized SCS
identities and the maximum SLSA Source level each SCS is trusted up to.
Different verifiers MAY use different roots of trust for repositories. The
root of trust configuration is likely in the form of a map from (SCS public
key identity, VSA <code>verifier.id</code>) to (SLSA Source level).</p>
<details>
<summary>Example root of trust configuration</summary>
<p>The following snippet shows conceptually how a verifier’s roots of trust
might be configured using made-up syntax.</p>
<div class="language-jsonc highlighter-rouge"><div class="highlight"><pre class="highlight"><code><span class="nl">"slsaSourceRootsOfTrust"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">[</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">// A SCS trusted at SLSA Source L3, using a fixed public key.</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"publicKey"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"HKJEwI..."</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"scsId"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://somescs.example.com/slsa/l3"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"slsaSourceLevel"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="mi">3</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">},</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">// A different SCS that claims to be SLSA Source L3,</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">// but this verifier only trusts it to L2.</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"publicKey"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"tLykq9..."</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"scsId"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://differentscs.example.com/slsa/l3"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"slsaSourceLevel"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="mi">2</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">},</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">// A SCS that uses Sigstore for authentication.</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"sigstore"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="p">{</span><span class="w">
            </span><span class="nl">"root"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"global"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">  </span><span class="c1">// identifies fulcio/rekor roots</span><span class="w">
            </span><span class="nl">"subjectAlternativeNamePattern"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://github.com/slsa-framework/slsa-source-poc/.github/workflows/compute_slsa_source.yml@refs/tags/v*.*.*"</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="p">},</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"scsId"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="s2">"https://github.com/slsa-framework/slsa-source-poc/.github/workflows/compute_slsa_source.yml@refs/tags/v*.*.*"</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
        </span><span class="nl">"slsaSourceLevel"</span><span class="p">:</span><span class="w"> </span><span class="mi">3</span><span class="p">,</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="p">}</span><span class="w">
    </span><span class="c1">...</span><span class="w">
</span><span class="p">]</span><span class="err">,</span><span class="w">
</span></code></pre></div></div>
</details>
</li>
</ul>
<p>Given a revision and its VSA follow the
<a href="#verification_summary-how-to-verify">VSA verification instructions</a> and the
<a href="https://github.com/in-toto/attestation/blob/main/docs/validation.md#validation-model">validation-model</a> using the revision identifier to perform subject matching and
checking the <code>verifier.id</code> against the root-of-trust described above.</p>
<h4 id="verifying-source-step-2-check-expectations">Step 2: Check Expectations</h4>
<p>Next, check that the revision’s VSA meets your expectations in order to mitigate
<a href="#threats-b-modifying-the-source">threat “B”</a>.</p>
<p>In our threat model, the adversary has the ability to create revisions within
the repository and get consumers to fetch that revision.  The adversary is not
able to subvert controls implemented by the Producer and enforced by the SCS.
Your expectations SHOULD be sufficient to detect an un-official revision and
SHOULD make it more difficult for an adversary to create a malicious official
revision.</p>
<p>You SHOULD compare the VSA against expected values for at least the following
fields:</p>
<table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>What</th>
<th>Why</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><code>verifier.id</code> identity from [Step 1]</td>
<td>To prevent an adversary from substituting a VSA making false claims from an unintended SCS.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code>subject.digest</code> from [Step 1]</td>
<td>To prevent an adversary from substituting a VSA from another revision.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code>verificationResult</code></td>
<td>To prevent an adversary from providing a VSA for a revision that failed some aspect of the organization’s expectations.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code>predicate.resourceUri</code></td>
<td>To prevent an adversary from substituting a VSA for the intended repository (e.g. <code>git+https://github.com/IntendedOrg/hello-world</code>) for another (e.g. <code>git+https://github.com/AdversaryOrg/hello-world</code>)</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code>subject.annotations.sourceRefs</code></td>
<td>To prevent an adversary from substituting the intended revision from one branch (e.g. <code>release</code>) with another (e.g. <code>experimental_auth</code>).</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><code>verifiedLevels</code></td>
<td>To ensure the expected controls were in place for the creation of the revision. E.g. <code>SLSA_SOURCE_LEVEL_3</code>, <code>ORG_SOURCE_STATIC_ANALYSIS</code>, etc…</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<h4 id="verifying-source-step-3-verify-evidence-using-source-provenance-optional">Step 3: Verify Evidence using Source Provenance [optional]</h4>
<p>Optionally, at SLSA Source Level 3 and up, check the <a href="#source-requirements-source-provenance-attestations">source provenance
attestations</a> directly.</p>
<p>As the format and implementation of source provenance attestations are left to
the SCS, you SHOULD form expectations about the claims in source provenance
attestations and how they map to a revision’s properties claimed in its VSA in
conjunction with the SCS and the producer.</p>
<h3 id="verifying-source-forming-expectations">Forming Expectations</h3>
<p><dfn>Expectations</dfn> are known values that indicate the corresponding
revision is authentic. For example, an SCS may maintain a mapping between
repository branches &amp; tags and the controls they claim to implement. That
mapping constitutes a set of expectations.</p>
<p>Possible models for forming expectations include:</p>
<ul>
<li>
<p><strong>Trust on first use:</strong> Accept the first version of the revision as-is. On
each update, compare the old VSA to the new VSA and alert on any
differences.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Defined by producer:</strong> The revision producer tells the verifier what their
expectations ought to be. In this model, the verifier SHOULD provide an
authenticated communication mechanism for the producer to set the revision’s
expectations, and there SHOULD be some protection against an adversary
unilaterally modifying them. For example, modifications might require
two-party control, or consumers might have to accept each policy change
(another form of trust on first use).</p>
</li>
</ul>
<p>It is important to note that expectations are tied to a <em>repository branch or
tag</em>, whereas a VSA is tied to an <em>revision</em>. Different revisions will have
different VSAs and the claims made by those VSAs may differ.</p>
<h3 id="verifying-source-architecture-options">Architecture options</h3>
<p>There are several options (non-mutually exclusive) for where VSA verification
can happen: the build system at source fetch time, the package ecosystem at
build artifact upload time, the consumers at download time, or
via a continuous monitoring system. Each option comes with its own set of
considerations, but all are valid and at least one SHOULD be used.</p>
<p>More than one component can verify VSAs. For example, even if a builder verifies
source VSAs, package ecosystems may wish to verify the source VSAs for the
artifacts they host that claim to be built from that source (as indicated by the
build provenance).</p>
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/verifying-source ends -->
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/assessing-source-systems begins -->
   <h2 id="assessing-source-systems">Source: Assessing source control systems</h2>
<p>One of SLSA’s guiding <a href="#principles">principles</a> is to “trust platforms, verify
artifacts”. However, consumers cannot trust source control systems (SCSs) unless
they have some proof that an SCS meets its
<a href="#source-requirements">requirements</a>.</p>
<p>This section describes the parts of an SCS that consumers SHOULD assess and
provides sample questions consumers can ask when assessing a SCS. See also
<a href="#threats">Threats &amp; mitigations</a>.</p>
<h3 id="assessing-source-systems-threats">Threats</h3>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-adversary-goal">Adversary goal</h4>
<p>The SLSA Source track defends against an adversary whose primary goal is to
inject unofficial behavior into protected source code while avoiding detection.
Organizations typically establish change management processes to prevent this
unofficial behavior from being introduced. The adversary’s goal is to bypass the
change management process.</p>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-adversary-profiles">Adversary profiles</h4>
<p>Consumers SHOULD also evaluate the source control systems’ ability to defend
against the following types of adversaries.</p>
<ol>
<li>Project contributors, who can:
<ul>
<li>Propose changes to protected branches of the source repo.</li>
<li>Upload new content to the source repo.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Project maintainer, who can:
<ul>
<li>Do everything listed under “project contributors”.</li>
<li>Define the purpose of the source repo.</li>
<li>Add or remove contributors to the source repo.</li>
<li>Add or remove permissions granted to contributors of the source repo.</li>
<li>Modify security controls within the source repo.</li>
<li>Modify controls used to enforce the change management process on the
source repo.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>Source control system administrators, who can:
<ul>
<li>Do everything listed under “project contributors” and “project
maintainers”.</li>
<li>Modify the source repo while bypassing the project maintainer’s controls.</li>
<li>Modify the behavior of the Source Control System itself.</li>
<li>Access the control plane’s cryptographic secrets.</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ol>
<h3 id="assessing-source-systems-source-control-system-components">Source Control System components</h3>
<p>Consumers SHOULD consider at least these elements when assessing a Source
Control System for SLSA conformance: control configuration, change management
interface, control plane, verifier, storage.</p>
<p><img src="/spec/v1.2/images/source-control-system-components.svg" alt="source control system components" /></p>
<p>The following subsection details these elements.</p>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-change-management-interface">Change management interface</h4>
<p>The change management interface is the user interface for proposing and
approving changes to protected branches within a source repository. During
normal operation all such changes go through this interface.</p>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-control-configuration">Control configuration</h4>
<p>Control configuration is how organizations establish technical controls in a
given source repository. If done well the configuration will reflect the intent
of the organization.</p>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-technical-controls">Technical controls</h4>
<p>Technical controls are the organization configured settings that are used to
determine if a revision can be introduced into storage within any particular
context and who has access to those revisions.</p>
<p>The technical controls component is responsible for the storage of these
settings while the <a href="#assessing-source-systems-control-plane">control plane</a> is responsible for enforcing
the configured controls.</p>
<p>They include:</p>
<ul>
<li>Read/write ACLs,</li>
<li>If approvals are required to introduce changes within a given context</li>
<li>Which actors are allowed to issue those approvals</li>
<li>Organization defined
<a href="#assessing-source-systems-enforced-change-management-process">change management processes</a>
requirements</li>
<li>etc…</li>
</ul>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-control-plane">Control plane</h4>
<p>The control plane is the SCS component that orchestrates the introduction and
creation of new revisions into a source repository. It is responsible for
enforcing <a href="#assessing-source-systems-technical-controls">technical controls</a> and, at SLSA Source L3+,
generating and signing source provenance for each revision. The control plane is
operated by one or more administrators, who have privileges to modify the
control plane.</p>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-verifier">Verifier</h4>
<p>The verifier is the SCS component that evaluates source provenance and generates
and signs a
<a href="#source-requirements-summary-attestation">verification summary attestation</a>
(VSA).</p>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-storage">Storage</h4>
<p>Storage holds source revisions and their provenance and summary attestations.</p>
<h3 id="assessing-source-systems-assessing-components">Assessing components</h3>
<p>The following are prompts for assessing a Source Control System’s ability to
meet the SLSA requirements.</p>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-prompts-for-assessing-the-change-management-interface">Prompts for assessing the change management interface</h4>
<ul>
<li>How does the SCS manage which actors are permitted to approve changes?</li>
<li>What types of non-plain-text changes can the change management interface
render? How well does the SCS render those changes?</li>
<li>What controls does the change management interface provide for enabling
Trusted Robot Contributions? Example: SLSA Build L3+ provenance, built from
SLSA Source L4+ source.</li>
</ul>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-prompts-for-assessing-control-configuration-amp-technical-controls">Prompts for assessing control configuration &amp; technical controls</h4>
<ul>
<li>How does the SCS prevent regression in control configurations?
Examples: built-in controls that cannot change, notifying project
maintainers when controls change, requiring public declaration of control
changes.</li>
<li>How does the SCS prevent individual project maintainers from tampering with
controls configured by the project?</li>
<li>How does the SCS prevent SCS administrators from tampering with a project’s
configured technical controls?</li>
</ul>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-prompts-for-assessing-the-control-plane-amp-verifier">Prompts for assessing the control plane &amp; verifier</h4>
<p>NOTE The control plane and verifier perform related roles within the SCS and
should typically be assessed together.</p>
<ul>
<li>
<p>Administration</p>
<ul>
<li>What are the ways an SCS administrator can use privileged access to
influence a revision creation, provenance generation, or VSA generation?
Examples: physical access, terminal access, access to cryptographic
secrets</li>
<li>What controls are in place to detect or prevent an SCS administrator
from abusing such access? Examples: two-person approvals, audit logging,
workload identities</li>
<li>Roughly how many SCS maintainers have such access?</li>
<li>How are privileged accounts protected? Examples: two-factor
authentication, client device security policies</li>
<li>What plans do you have for recovering from security incidents and
platform outages? Are they tested? How frequently?</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Control effectiveness</p>
<ul>
<li>How does the SCS ensure the control plane is enforcing the
<a href="#assessing-source-systems-technical-controls">technical controls</a> are working as intended?</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Source provenance generation</p>
<ul>
<li>How does the control plane observe the revision creation to ensure the
provenance’s accuracy?</li>
<li>Are there situations in which the control plane will not generate
source provenance? What are they?</li>
<li>What details are included in the source provenance? Are they sufficient
to mitigate tampering with other SCS components?</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>VSA generation</p>
<ul>
<li>How does the verifier determine what source level a revision meets?</li>
<li>How does the verifier determine the organization’s control expectations
and if they are met?</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Development practices</p>
<ul>
<li>How do you track the control plane and verifier’s software and
configuration?
Example: version control.</li>
<li>How do you build confidence in the control plane’s software supply
chain? Example: SLSA Build L3+ provenance, built from SLSA Source L4+
source.</li>
<li>How do you secure communications between components? Example: TLS with
certificate transparency.</li>
</ul>
</li>
<li>
<p>Managing cryptographic secrets</p>
<ul>
<li>How do you store the control plane and verifier’s cryptographic secrets?</li>
<li>Which parts of the organization have access to the control plane and
verifier’s cryptographic secrets?</li>
<li>What controls are in place to detect or prevent SCS administrators from
abusing such access? Examples: two-person approvals, audit logging</li>
<li>How are secrets protected in memory? Examples: secrets are stored in
hardware security modules and backed up in secure cold storage</li>
<li>How frequently are cryptographic secrets rotated? Describe the rotation
process.</li>
<li>What is your plan for remediating cryptographic secret compromise? How
frequently is this plan tested?</li>
</ul>
</li>
</ul>
<h4 id="assessing-source-systems-prompts-for-assessing-output-storage">Prompts for assessing output storage</h4>
<ul>
<li>How do you prevent tampering with storage directly?</li>
<li>How do you prevent one project’s revisions from affecting another project?</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="assessing-source-systems-source-control-system-evaluation">Source control system evaluation</h3>
<p>Organizations can either self-attest to their answers or seek certification from
a third-party auditor. Evidence for self-attestation should be published on
the internet and can include information such as the security model used in the
evaluation. Evidence submitted for third-party certification need not be
published.</p>
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/assessing-source-systems ends -->
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/source-example-controls begins -->
   <h2 id="source-example-controls">Source: Example controls</h2>
<p>At SLSA Source L3+ organizations are allowed and encouraged to define their own
controls that go over and above specific requirements outlined by SLSA. This
page provides some examples of what these additional controls could be.</p>
<p>If an organization has indicated that use of these controls is part of
their repository’s expectations, consumers SHOULD be able to verify that the
process was followed for the revision they are consuming by examining the
<a href="./source-requirements#source-verification-summary-attestation">summary</a> or
<a href="./source-requirements#source-provenance-attestations">source provenance</a>
attestations.</p>
<blockquote>
<p>For example: consumers can look for the related <code>ORG_SOURCE</code> properties in
the <code>verifiedLevels</code> field of the <a href="./source-requirements#source-verification-summary-attestation">summary
attestation</a>.</p>
</blockquote>
<h3 id="source-example-controls-expert-code-review">Expert Code Review</h3>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>All changes to the source are pre-approved by experts.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>Enterprise repositories and mature open source projects.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>Prevents mistakes by developers unfamiliar with the area.</p>
</dl>
<h4 id="source-example-controls-requirements">Requirements</h4>
<ul>
<li>
<p><strong>Code ownership</strong></p>
<p>Each part of the source MUST have a clearly identified set of experts.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Approvals from all relevant experts</strong></p>
<p>For each portion of the source modified by a change proposal, pre-approval
MUST be granted by a member of the defined expert set. An approval from an
actor that is a member of multiple expert groups may satisfy the
requirement for all groups in which they are a member.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="source-example-controls-review-every-single-revision">Review Every Single Revision</h3>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>The final revision was reviewed by experts prior to submission.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>The highest-of-high-security-posture repos.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>Provides maximum chance for experts to spot problems.</p>
</dl>
<h4 id="source-example-controls-requirements-1">Requirements</h4>
<ul>
<li>
<p><strong>Reset votes on all changes</strong></p>
<p>If the proposal is modified after receiving expert approval, all previously
granted approvals MUST be revoked. A new approval MUST be granted from ALL
required reviewers.</p>
<p>The new approval MAY be granted by an actor who approved a previous
iteration.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="source-example-controls-automated-testing">Automated testing</h3>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>The final revision was validated by automated tests.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>All organizations and repositories.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>Improves accuracy, prevents errors, and reduces human load.</p>
</dl>
<h4 id="source-example-controls-requirements-2">Requirements</h4>
<p>The organization MUST configure a branch protection rule to require that only
revisions with passing test results can be pointed-to by the branch.</p>
<p>Automatic tests SHOULD be executed in a trustworthy environment (see SLSA
build track).</p>
<p>Results of each test (or an aggregate) MUST be collected by the change review
tool and made available for verification.</p>
<p>Tests SHOULD be run against a revision created for testing by merging the topic
branch (containing the proposed changes) into the target branch.</p>
<p>Use of the proposed merge commit should be preferred to using the tip of the
topic branch.</p>
<h3 id="source-example-controls-every-revision-reachable-from-a-branch-was-approved">Every revision reachable from a branch was approved</h3>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>New revisions are created based ONLY on approved changes.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>All organizations and repositories.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>Prevents attacks that hide malicious, unreviewed commits.</p>
</dl>
<h4 id="source-example-controls-context">Context</h4>
<p>In many organizations, it is normal to review only the “net difference”
between the tip of the topic branch and the “best merge base”, the closest
shared commit between the topic and target branches computed at the time of
review.</p>
<p>The topic branch may contain many commits of which not all were intended to
represent a shippable state of the repository.</p>
<p>If a repository merges branches with a standard merge commit, all those
unreviewed commits on the topic branch will become “reachable” from the
protected branch by virtue of the multi-parent merge commit.</p>
<p>When a repo is cloned, all commits <em>reachable</em> from the main branch are
fetched and become accessible from the local checkout.</p>
<p>This combination of factors allows attacks where the victim performs a <code>git clone</code> operation followed by a <code>git reset --hard &lt;unreviewed revision ID&gt;</code>.</p>
<h4 id="source-example-controls-requirements-3">Requirements</h4>
<ul>
<li>
<p><strong>Informed Review</strong></p>
<p>The reviewer is able and encouraged to make an informed decision about
what they’re approving. The reviewer MUST be presented with a full,
meaningful content diff between the proposed revision and the
previously reviewed revision.</p>
<p>It is not sufficient to indicate that a file changed without showing
the contents.</p>
</li>
<li>
<p><strong>Use only rebase operations on the protected branch</strong></p>
<p>Require a squash merge strategy for the protected branch.</p>
<p>To guarantee that only commits representing reviewed diffs are cloned,
the SCS MUST rebase (or “squash”) the reviewed diff into a single new
commit (the “squashed” commit) that has only a single parent (the
revision previously pointed-to by the protected branch). This is
different than a standard merge commit strategy which would cause all
the user-contributed commits to become reachable from the protected
branch via the second parent.</p>
<p>It is not acceptable to replay the sequence of commits from the topic
branch onto the protected branch. The intent is to reduce the accepted
changes to the exact diffs that were reviewed. Constituent commits of
the topic branch may or may not have been reviewed on an individual
basis, and should not become reachable from the protected branch.</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="source-example-controls-immutable-change-discussion">Immutable Change Discussion</h3>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>The discussion around a change is preserved and immutable.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>Large orgs, or where discussion is vital to change management.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>Enables future education, forensics, and security auditing.</p>
</dl>
<h4 id="source-example-controls-requirements-4">Requirements</h4>
<p>The SCS SHOULD record a description of the proposed change and all discussions
/ commentary related to it.</p>
<p>The SCS MUST link this discussion to the revision itself. This is regularly
done via commit metadata.</p>
<p>All collected content SHOULD be made immutable if the change is accepted. It
SHOULD NOT be possible to edit the discussion around a revision after it has
been accepted.</p>
<h3 id="source-example-controls-merge-trains">Merge trains</h3>
<dl class="as-table">
<dt>Summary<dd>
<p>A buffer branch (or “train”) collects a certain number of approved changes
before merging into the protected branch.</p>
<dt>Intended for<dd>
<p>Large organizations with high-velocity repositories where the protected branch
needs to remain stable for longer periods.</p>
<dt>Benefits<dd>
<p>Allows more time for human and automatic code review by stabilizing the
protected branch.</p>
</dl>
<h4 id="source-example-controls-requirements-5">Requirements</h4>
<p>Large organizations must keep the number of updates to key protected branches
under certain limits to allow time for code review to happen. For example, if
a team tries to merge 60 change requests per hour into the <code>main</code> branch, the
tip of the <code>main</code> branch would only be stable for about 1 minute. This would
leave only 1 minute for a new diff to be both generated and reviewed before
it becomes stale again.</p>
<p>The normal way to work in this environment is to create a buffer branch
(sometimes called a “train”) to collect a certain number of approved changes.
In this model, when a change is approved for submission to the protected
branch, it is added to the train branch instead. After a certain amount of
time, the train branch will be merged into the protected branch. If there are
problems detected with the contents on the train branch, it’s normal for the
whole train to be abandoned and a new train to be formed. Approved changes
will be re-applied to a new train in this scenario.</p>
<p>The key benefit to this approach is that the protected branch remains stable
for longer, allowing more time for human and automatic code review. A key
downside to this approach is that organizations will not know the final
revision ID that represents a change until the entire train process completes.</p>
<p>A change review process will now be associated with multiple distinct
revisions.</p>
<ul>
<li>ID 1: The revision which was reviewed before concluding the change review
process. It represents the ideal state of the protected branch applying
only this proposed change.</li>
<li>ID 2: The revision created when the change is applied to the train branch.
It represents the state of the protected branch <em>after other changes have
been applied</em>.</li>
</ul>
<p>It is important to note that no human or automatic review will have the chance
to pre-approve ID2. This will appear to violate any organization policies that
require pre-approval of changes before submission. The SCS and the
organization MUST protect this process in the same way they protect other
artifact build pipelines.</p>
<p>At a minimum the SCS MUST issue an attestation that the revision ID generated
by a merged train is identical (“MERGESAME” in git terminology) to the state
of the protected branch after applying each approved changeset in sequence.
No other content may be added or removed during this process.</p>
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/source-example-controls ends -->
   <!-- /spec/v1.2/threats begins -->
   <h2 id="threats">Threats & mitigations</h2>
<p>What follows is a comprehensive technical analysis of supply chain threats and
their corresponding mitigations with SLSA and other best practices. For an
introduction to the supply chain threats that SLSA is aiming to protect
against, see <a href="#threats-overview">Supply chain threats</a>.</p>
<p>The examples on this section are meant to:</p>
<ul>
<li>Explain the reasons for each of the SLSA <a href="#build-requirements">build</a> and
<a href="#source-requirements">source</a> requirements.</li>
<li>Increase confidence that the SLSA requirements are sufficient to achieve the
desired <a href="#about-how-slsa-works">level</a> of integrity protection.</li>
<li>Help implementers better understand what they are protecting against so that
they can better design and implement controls.</li>
</ul>
<!--
**TODO:** Expand this threat model to also cover "unknowns". Not sure if that is
a "threat" or a "risk". Example: If libFoo is compromised, how do you know if
you are compromised? At a first level, if you don't even know whether you
include libFoo or not, that's a big risk. But even then, it might be that you
don't use libFoo in a way that makes your product vulnerable. We should capture
that somehow. This isn't specific to dependencies - it applies to the entire
diagram.
([discussion](https://github.com/slsa-framework/slsa/pull/1046/files/ebf34a8f9e874b219f152bad62673eae0b3ba2c3#r1585440922))
-->
<article class="threats">
<h3 id="threats-overview">Overview</h3>
<p><img src="/spec/v1.2/images/supply-chain-threats.svg" alt="Supply Chain Threats" /></p>
<p>This threat model covers the <em>software supply chain</em>, meaning the process by
which software is produced and consumed. We describe and cluster threats based
on where in the software development pipeline those threats occur, labeled (A)
through (I). This is useful because priorities and mitigations mostly cluster
along those same lines. Keep in mind that dependencies are
<a href="#threats-dependency-threats">highly recursive</a>, so each dependency has its own threats
(A) through (I), and the same for <em>their</em> dependencies, and so on. For a more
detailed explanation of the supply chain model, see
<a href="#terminology">Terminology</a>.</p>
<p>Importantly, producers and consumers face <em>aggregate</em> risk across all of the
software they produce and consume, respectively. Many organizations produce
and/or consume thousands of software packages, both first- and third-party, and
it is not practical to rely on every individual team in the organization to do
the right thing. For this reason, SLSA prioritizes mitigations that can be
broadly adopted in an automated fashion, minimizing the chance of mistakes.</p>
<h3 id="threats-source-threats">Source threats</h3>
<p>A source integrity threat is a potential for an adversary to introduce a change
to the source code that does not reflect the intent of the software producer.
This includes modification of the source data at rest as well as insider threats,
when an authorized individual introduces an unauthorized change.</p>
<p>The SLSA Source track mitigates these threats when the consumer
<a href="#verifying-source">verifies source</a> against expectations, confirming
that the revision they received was created in the expected manner.</p>
<h4 id="threats-a-producer">(A) Producer</h4>
<p>The producer of the software intentionally produces code that harms the
consumer, or the producer otherwise uses practices that are not deserving of the
consumer’s trust.</p>
<details><summary>Software producer intentionally creates a malicious revision of the source</summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> A producer intentionally creates a malicious revision with the intent of harming their consumers.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em>
This kind of attack cannot be directly mitigated through SLSA controls.
Consumers must establish some basis to trust the organizations from which they consume software.
That basis may be:</p>
<ul>
<li>The repo is open source with an active user-base. High numbers of engaged users may increase the likelihood that bad code is detected during code review and reduce the time-to-detection when bad revisions are accepted.</li>
<li>The organization has sufficient legal or reputational incentives to dissuade it from making malicious changes.</li>
</ul>
<p>Ultimately this is a judgement call with no straightforward answer.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> A producer with an otherwise good reputation decides suddenly to produce a malicious artifact with the intent to harm their consumers.</p>
</details>
<h4 id="threats-b-modifying-the-source">(B) Modifying the source</h4>
<p>An adversary without any special administrator privileges attempts to introduce a change counter to the declared intent of the source by following the producer’s official source control process.</p>
<p>Threats in this category can be mitigated by following source control management best practices.</p>
<h5 id="threats-b1-submit-change-without-review">(B1) Submit change without review</h5>
<details><summary>Directly submit without review<span>(Source L4)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Malicious code submitted to the source repository.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Require approval of all changes before they are accepted.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary directly pushes a change to a git repo’s <code>main</code> branch.
Solution: The Source Control System is configured to require two party review for
contributions to the <code>main</code> branch.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Single actor controls multiple accounts</summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> An actor is able to control multiple account and effectively approve their own code changes.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The producer must ensure that no actor is able to control or influence multiple accounts with review privileges.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary creates a pull request using a secondary account and approves it using their primary account.
Solution: The producer must track all actors who have both explicit review permissions and the independent ability to control
a privileged bot. A common vector for this attack is to influence a robot account with the permission to review or contribute
code. Control of the robot account and an actor’s own personal account is enough to exploit this vulnerability. A common
solution to this flow is to deny bot accounts from contributing or reviewing code, or to require more human reviews in those
cases.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Use a robot account to submit change</summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Exploit a robot account that has the ability to submit changes without
two-person review.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> All changes require review by two people, even changes authored by
robots.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> A file within the source repository is automatically generated by a
robot, which is allowed to submit without review.
Adversary compromises the robot and submits a malicious change.
Solution: Require two-person review for such changes, ignoring the robot.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Abuse of rule exceptions</summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Rule exceptions provide vector for abuse.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Remove rule exceptions.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> A producer intends to require two-person review on “all changes except for documentation changes,” defined as those only modifying <code>.md</code> files.
Adversary submits a malicious executable masquerading as a documentation file, <code>help.md</code>.
This avoids the two-person review rule due to the exception.
In the future, a user (or another workflow) can be induced to <em>execute</em> <code>help.md</code> and become compromised.
Technically the malicious code change met all defined policies yet the intent of the organization was defeated.
Solution: The producer adjusts the rules to prohibit such exceptions.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Highly-permissioned actor bypasses or disables controls<span>(verification)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Trusted actor with “admin” privileges in a repository submits code by disabling existing controls.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The Source Control System must have controls in place to prevent
and detect abusive behavior from administrators (e.g. two-person approvals,
audit logging).</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> GitHub repository-level admin removes a branch protection requirement, pushes
their change, then re-enables the requirement to cover their tracks.
Solution: Consumers do not accept claims from the Source Control System unless
they trust sufficient controls are in place to prevent repo admins from
abusing privileges.</p>
</details>
<h5 id="threats-b2-evade-change-management-process">(B2) Evade change management process</h5>
<details><summary>Alter change history<span>(Source L2+)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Adversary alters branch history to hide malicious activity.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The Source Control System prevents branch history from being
altered.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary submits a malicious commit <code>X</code> to the <code>main</code> branch. A
release is built and published from <code>X</code>. The adversary then “force pushes”
to <code>main</code> erasing the record of the malicious commit.  Solution: The Source
Control System is configured to prevent force pushes to <code>main</code>.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Replace tagged content with malicious content<span>(Source L2+)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Adversary alters a tag to point at malicious content.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The Source Control System does not allow protected tags to be updated.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary crafts a malicious commit <code>X</code> on a development branch which
does enforce any controls. They then update the <code>release_1.2</code> tag to point to
<code>X</code>. Consumers of <code>release_1.2</code> will get the malicious revision. Solution: The
Source Control System does not allow protected tags to be updated.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Skip required checks<span>(Source L3+)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Code is submitted without following the producers documented
development process, introducing unintended behavior.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The producer uses the Source Control System to implement technical
controls ensuring adherence to the development process.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> An engineer submits a new feature that has a critical flaw on an
untested code path, in violation of the producer’s documented process of having
high test coverage. Solution: The producer implements a technical control in the
SCS that requires 95%+ test coverage.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Modify code after review<span>(Source L4)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Modify the code after it has been reviewed but before submission.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The Source Control System invalidates approvals whenever the proposed change is modified.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Source repository requires two-person review on all changes.
Adversary sends an initial “good” pull request to a peer, who approves it.
Adversary then modifies their proposal to contain “bad” code.</p>
<p>Solution: Configure the code review rules to require review of the most recent revision before submission.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Submit a change that is unreviewable<span>(Source L4)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Adversary crafts a change that looks benign to a reviewer but is actually malicious.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Code review system ensures that all reviews are informed and
meaningful to the extent possible. For example the system could show
&amp; resolve symlinks, render images, or verify &amp; display provenance.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> A proposed change updates a JPEG file to include a malicious
message, but the reviewer is only presented with a diff of the binary
file contents. The reviewer is unable to parse the contents themselves
so they do not have enough context to provide a meaningful review.
Solution: the code review system should present the reviewer with a
rendering of the image and the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exif">embedded
metadata</a>, allowing them to make an
informed decision.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Copy a reviewed change to another context<span>(Source L4)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Get a change reviewed in one context and then transfer it to a
different context.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Approvals are context-specific.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> MyPackage’s source repository requires two-person review. Adversary
forks the repo, submits a change in the fork with review from a colluding
colleague (who is not trusted by MyPackage), then proposes the change to
the upstream repo.
Solution: The proposed change still requires two-person review in the upstream
context even though it received two-person review in another context.</p>
</details>
</details>
<details><summary>Commit graph attacks</summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> A malicious commit can be included in a sequence of commits such that it does not appear malicious in the net change presented to reviewers.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The producer ensures that all revisions in the protected context followed the same contribution process.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary sends a pull request containing malicious commit X and a commit Y that undoes X.
The combined change of X + Y displays zero lines of malicious code and the reviewer cannot tell that X is malicious unless they review it individually.
If X is allowed to become reachable from the protected branch, the content may become available in secured environments such as developer machines.</p>
<p>Solution: Each revision in the protected context must have followed the intended process.
Ultimately, this means that either each code review results in at most a single new commit or that the full process is followed for each constituent commit in a proposed sequence.</p>
</details>
<h5 id="threats-b3-render-code-review-ineffective">(B3) Render code review ineffective</h5>
<details><summary>Collude with another trusted person</summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Two trusted persons collude to author and approve a bad change.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> This threat is not currently addressed by SLSA, but the producer can arbitrarily increase friction of their policies to reduce risk, such as requiring additional, or more senior reviewers.
The goal of policy here is to ensure that the approved changes match the intention of the producer for the source.
Increasing the friction of the policies may make it harder to circumvent, but doing so has diminishing returns.
Ultimately the producer will need to land upon a balanced risk profile that makes sense for their security posture.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Trick reviewer into approving bad code</summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Construct a change that looks benign but is actually malicious, a.k.a.
“bugdoor.”</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> This threat is not currently addressed by SLSA.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Reviewer blindly approves changes</summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Reviewer approves changes without actually reviewing, a.k.a. “rubber
stamping.”</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> This threat is not currently addressed by SLSA.</p>
</details>
<h5 id="threats-b4-render-change-metadata-ineffective">(B4) Render change metadata ineffective</h5>
<details><summary>Forge change metadata<span>(Source L2+)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Forge the change metadata to alter attribution, timestamp, or
discoverability of a change.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The Source Control System only attributes changes to authenticated
identities and records contemporaneous evidence of changes in signed source
provenance attestations.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary ‘X’ creates a commit with unauthenticated metadata claiming
it was authored by ‘Y’. Solution: The Source Control System records the identity
of ‘X’ when ‘X’ submits the commit to the repository.</p>
</details>
<h4 id="threats-c-source-code-management">(C) Source code management</h4>
<p>An adversary introduces a change to the source control repository through an
administrative interface, or through a compromise of the underlying
infrastructure.</p>
<details><summary>Platform admin abuses privileges<span>(verification)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Platform administrator abuses their privileges to bypass controls or
to push a malicious version of the software.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The source platform must have controls in place to prevent and
detect abusive behavior from administrators (e.g. two-person approvals for
changes to the infrastructure, audit logging). A future <a href="#future-directions-platform-operations-track">Platform
Operations Track</a> may provide
more specific guidance on how to secure the underlying platform.</p>
<p><em>Example 1:</em> GitHostingService employee uses an internal tool to push changes to
the MyPackage source repo.</p>
<p><em>Example 2:</em> GitHostingService employee uses an internal tool to push a
malicious version of the server to serve malicious versions of MyPackage sources
to a specific CI/CD client but the regular version to everyone else, in order to
hide tracks.</p>
<p><em>Example 3:</em> GitHostingService employee uses an internal tool to push a
malicious version of the server that includes a backdoor allowing specific users
to bypass branch protections. Adversary then uses this backdoor to submit a
change to MyPackage without review.</p>
<p><em>Solution:</em> Consumers do not accept claims from the Source Control System unless
they trust sufficient controls are in place to prevent repo admins from
abusing privileges.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Exploit vulnerability in SCM</summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Exploit a vulnerability in the implementation of the source code
management system to bypass controls.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> This threat is not currently addressed by SLSA.</p>
</details>
<h3 id="threats-build-threats">Build threats</h3>
<p>A build integrity threat is a potential for an adversary to introduce behavior
to an artifact without changing its source code, or to build from a
source, dependency, and/or process that is not intended by the software
producer.</p>
<p>The SLSA Build track mitigates these threats when the consumer
<a href="#verifying-artifacts">verifies artifacts</a> against expectations, confirming
that the artifact they received was built in the expected manner.</p>
<h4 id="threats-d-external-build-parameters">(D) External build parameters</h4>
<p>An adversary builds from a version of the source code that does not match the
official source control repository, or changes the build parameters to inject
behavior that was not intended by the official source.</p>
<p>The mitigation here is to compare the provenance against expectations for the
package, which depends on SLSA Build L1 for provenance. (Threats against the
provenance itself are covered by (E) and (F).)</p>
<details><summary>Build from unofficial fork of code <span>(expectations)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Build using the expected CI/CD process but from an unofficial fork of
the code that may contain unauthorized changes.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier requires the provenance’s source location to match an
expected value.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> MyPackage is supposed to be built from GitHub repo <code>good/my-package</code>.
Instead, it is built from <code>evilfork/my-package</code>. Solution: Verifier rejects
because the source location does not match.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Build from unofficial branch or tag <span>(expectations)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Build using the expected CI/CD process and source location, but
checking out an “experimental” branch or similar that may contain code not
intended for release.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier requires that the provenance’s source branch/tag matches
an expected value, or that the source revision is reachable from an expected
branch.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> MyPackage’s releases are tagged from the <code>main</code> branch, which has
branch protections. Adversary builds from the unprotected <code>experimental</code> branch
containing unofficial changes. Solution: Verifier rejects because the source
revision is not reachable from <code>main</code>.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Build from unofficial build steps <span>(expectations)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Build the package using the proper CI/CD platform but with unofficial
build steps.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier requires that the provenance’s build configuration source
matches an expected value.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> MyPackage is expected to be built by Google Cloud Build using the
build steps defined in the source’s <code>cloudbuild.yaml</code> file. Adversary builds
with Google Cloud Build, but using custom build steps provided over RPC.
Solution: Verifier rejects because the build steps did not come from the
expected source.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Build from unofficial parameters <span>(expectations)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Build using the expected CI/CD process, source location, and
branch/tag, but using a parameter that injects unofficial behavior.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier requires that the provenance’s external parameters all
match expected values.</p>
<p><em>Example 1:</em> MyPackage is supposed to be built from the <code>release.yml</code> workflow.
Adversary builds from the <code>debug.yml</code> workflow. Solution: Verifier rejects
because the workflow parameter does not match the expected value.</p>
<p><em>Example 2:</em> MyPackage’s GitHub Actions Workflow uses <code>github.event.inputs</code> to
allow users to specify custom compiler flags per invocation. Adversary sets a
compiler flag that overrides a macro to inject malicious behavior into the
output binary. Solution: Verifier rejects because the <code>inputs</code> parameter was not
expected.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Build from modified version of code modified after checkout <span>(expectations)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Build from a version of the code that includes modifications after
checkout.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Build platform pulls directly from the source repository and
accurately records the source location in provenance.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary fetches from MyPackage’s source repo, makes a local commit,
then requests a build from that local commit. Builder records the fact that it
did not pull from the official source repo. Solution: Verifier rejects because
the source repo does not match the expected value.</p>
</details>
<h4 id="threats-e-build-process">(E) Build process</h4>
<p>An adversary introduces an unauthorized change to a build output through
tampering of the build process; or introduces false information into the
provenance.</p>
<p>These threats are directly addressed by the SLSA Build track.</p>
<details><summary>Forge values of the provenance (other than output digest) <span>(Build L2+)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Generate false provenance and get the trusted control plane to sign
it.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> At Build L2+, the trusted control plane <a href="#build-requirements-provenance-authentic">generates</a> all
information that goes in the provenance, except (optionally) the output artifact
hash. At Build L3+, this is <a href="#build-requirements-provenance-unforgeable">hardened</a> to prevent compromise even
by determined adversaries.</p>
<p><em>Example 1 (Build L2):</em> Provenance is generated on the build worker, which the
adversary has control over. Adversary uses a malicious process to get the build
platform to claim that it was built from source repo <code>good/my-package</code> when it
was really built from <code>evil/my-package</code>. Solution: Builder generates and signs
the provenance in the trusted control plane; the worker reports the output
artifacts but otherwise has no influence over the provenance.</p>
<p><em>Example 2 (Build L3):</em> Provenance is generated in the trusted control plane,
but workers can break out of the container to access the signing material.
Solution: Builder is hardened to provide strong isolation against tenant
projects.</p>
</details>
<details id="forged-digest"><summary>Forge output digest of the provenance <span>(n/a)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> The tenant-controlled build process sets output artifact digest
(<code>subject</code> in SLSA Provenance) without the trusted control plane verifying that
such an artifact was actually produced.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> None; this is not a problem. Any build claiming to produce a given
artifact could have actually produced it by copying it verbatim from input to
output.<sup class="footnote-ref"><a href="#threats-fn1" id="fnref1">1</a></sup> (Reminder: Provenance is only a claim that a particular
artifact was <em>built</em>, not that it was <em>published</em> to a particular registry.)</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> A legitimate MyPackage artifact has digest <code>abcdef</code> and is built
from source repo <code>good/my-package</code>. A malicious build from source repo
<code>evil/my-package</code> claims that it built artifact <code>abcdef</code> when it did not.
Solution: Verifier rejects because the source location does not match; the
forged digest is irrelevant.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Compromise project owner <span>(Build L2+)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> An adversary gains owner permissions for the artifact’s build project.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The build project owner must not have the ability to influence the
build process or provenance generation.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> MyPackage is built on Awesome Builder under the project “mypackage”.
Adversary is an owner of the “mypackage” project. Awesome Builder allows
owners to debug the build environment via SSH. An adversary uses this feature
to alter a build in progress. Solution: Build L3 requires the external parameters
to be complete in the provenance. The attackers access and/or actions within the
SSH connection would be enumerated within the external parameters. The updated
external parameters will not match the declared expectations causing verification
to fail.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Compromise other build <span>(Build L3)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Perform a malicious build that alters the behavior of a benign
build running in parallel or subsequent environments.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Builds are <a href="#build-requirements-isolated">isolated</a> from one another, with no way for one to
affect the other or persist changes.</p>
<p><em>Example 1:</em> A build platform runs all builds for project MyPackage on
the same machine as the same Linux user. An adversary starts a malicious build
that listens for another build and swaps out source files, then starts a benign
build. The benign build uses the malicious build’s source files, but its
provenance says it used benign source files. Solution: The build platform
changes architecture to isolate each build in a separate VM or similar.</p>
<p><em>Example 2:</em> A build platform uses the same machine for subsequent
builds. An adversary first runs a build that replaces the <code>make</code> binary with a
malicious version, then subsequently runs an otherwise benign build. Solution:
The builder changes architecture to start each build with a clean machine image.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Steal cryptographic secrets <span>(Build L3)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Use or exfiltrate the provenance signing key or some other
cryptographic secret that should only be available to the build platform.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Builds are <a href="#build-requirements-isolated">isolated</a> from the trusted build platform control
plane, and only the control plane has <a href="#build-requirements-provenance-unforgeable">access</a> to cryptographic
secrets.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Provenance is signed on the build worker, which the adversary has
control over. Adversary uses a malicious process that generates false provenance
and signs it using the provenance signing key. Solution: Builder generates and
signs provenance in the trusted control plane; the worker has no access to the
key.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Poison the build cache <span>(Build L3)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Add a malicious artifact to a build cache that is later picked up by a
benign build process (<a href="https://adnanthekhan.com/2024/05/06/the-monsters-in-your-build-cache-github-actions-cache-poisoning/">example</a>).</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Build caches must be <a href="#build-requirements-isolated">isolated</a> between builds to prevent
such cache poisoning attacks. In particular, the cache SHOULD be keyed by the
transitive closure of all inputs to the cached artifact, and the cache must
either be only writable by the trusted control plane or have SLSA Build L3
provenance for each cache entry.</p>
<p><em>Example 1:</em> The cache key does not fully cover the transitive closure of all
inputs and instead only uses the digest of the source file itself. Adversary runs
a build over <code>auth.cc</code> with command line flags to gcc that define a marco
replacing <code>CheckAuth(ctx)</code> with <code>true</code>. When subsequent builds build <code>auth.cc</code>
they will get the attacker’s poisoned instance that does not call <code>CheckAuth</code>.
Solution: Build cache is keyed by digest of <code>auth.cc</code>, command line, and digest of
gcc so changing the command line flags results in a different cache entry.</p>
<p><em>Example 2:</em> The tenant controlled build process has full write access to the
cache. Adversary observes a legitimate build of <code>auth.cc</code> which covers the
transitive closure of all inputs and notes the digest used for caching. The
adversary builds a malicious version of <code>auth.o</code> and directly writes it to the
build cache using the observed digest. Subsequent legitimate builds will use
the malicious version of <code>auth.o</code>. Solution: Each cache entry is keyed by the
transitive closure of the inputs, and the cache entry is itself a SLSA Build L3
build with its own provenance that corresponds to the key.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Compromise build platform admin <span>(verification)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> An adversary gains admin permissions for the artifact’s build platform.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> The build platform must have controls in place to prevent and
detect abusive behavior from administrators (e.g. two-person approvals, audit
logging).</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> MyPackage is built on Awesome Builder. Awesome Builder allows
engineers on-call to SSH into build machines to debug production issues. An
adversary uses this access to modify a build in progress. Solution: Consumers
do not accept provenance from the build platform unless they trust sufficient
controls are in place to prevent abusing admin privileges.</p>
</details>
<h4 id="threats-f-artifact-publication">(F) Artifact publication</h4>
<p>An adversary uploads a package artifact that does not reflect the intent of the
package’s official source control repository.</p>
<p>This is the most direct threat because it is the easiest to pull off. If there
are no mitigations for this threat, then (D) and (E) are often indistinguishable
from this threat.</p>
<!--
**TODO:** We need to define "official source control repository". Its meaning is
not obvious. The gist is that each package theoretically has some "official" or
"canonical" repository from which it "should" be built, and the attack here is
that you either build from a different source repository or otherwise do
something that doesn't reflect that source repository. But we need to nail down
this concept.
-->
<details><summary>Build with untrusted CI/CD <span>(expectations)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Build using an unofficial CI/CD pipeline that does not build in the
correct way.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier requires provenance showing that the builder matched an
expected value.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> MyPackage is expected to be built on Google Cloud Build, which is
trusted up to Build L3. Adversary builds on SomeOtherBuildPlatform, which is only
trusted up to Build L2, and then exploits SomeOtherBuildPlatform to inject
malicious behavior. Solution: Verifier rejects because builder is not as
expected.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Upload package without provenance <span>(Build L1)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Upload a package without provenance.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier requires provenance before accepting the package.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary uploads a malicious version of MyPackage to the package
repository without provenance. Solution: Verifier rejects because provenance is
missing.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Tamper with artifact after CI/CD <span>(Build L1)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Take a benign version of the package, modify it in some way, then
re-upload it using the original provenance.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier checks that the provenance’s <code>subject</code> matches the hash
of the package.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary performs a proper build, modifies the artifact, then
uploads the modified version of the package to the repository along with the
provenance. Solution: Verifier rejects because the hash of the artifact does not
match the <code>subject</code> found within the provenance.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Tamper with provenance <span>(Build L2)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Perform a build that would not meet expectations, then modify the
provenance to make the expectations checks pass.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier only accepts provenance with a valid <a href="#build-requirements-provenance-authentic">cryptographic
signature</a> or equivalent proving that the provenance came from an
acceptable builder.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> MyPackage is expected to be built by GitHub Actions from the
<code>good/my-package</code> repo. Adversary builds with GitHub Actions from the
<code>evil/my-package</code> repo and then modifies the provenance so that the source looks
like it came from <code>good/my-package</code>. Solution: Verifier rejects because the
cryptographic signature is no longer valid.</p>
</details>
<h4 id="threats-g-distribution-channel">(G) Distribution channel</h4>
<p>An adversary modifies the package on the package registry using an
administrative interface or through a compromise of the infrastructure
including modification of the package in transit to the consumer.</p>
<p>The distribution channel threats and mitigations look very similar to the
Artifact Publication (F) threats and mitigations with the main difference
being that these threats are mitigated by having the <em>consumer</em> perform
verification.</p>
<p>The consumer’s actions may be simplified if (F) produces a <a href="#verification_summary">VSA</a>.
In this case the consumer may replace provenance verification with
<a href="#verification_summary-how-to-verify">VSA verification</a>.</p>
<details><summary>Build with untrusted CI/CD <span>(expectations)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Replace the package with one built using an unofficial CI/CD pipeline
that does not build in the correct way.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier requires provenance showing that the builder matched an
expected value or a VSA for corresponding <code>resourceUri</code>.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> MyPackage is expected to be built on Google Cloud Build, which is
trusted up to Build L3. Adversary builds on SomeOtherBuildPlatform, which is only
trusted up to Build L2, and then exploits SomeOtherBuildPlatform to inject
malicious behavior. Adversary then replaces the original package within the
repository with the malicious package. Solution: Verifier rejects because
builder is not as expected.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Issue VSA from untrusted intermediary <span>(expectations)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Have an unofficial intermediary issue a VSA for a malicious package.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation</em>: Verifier requires VSAs to be issued by a trusted intermediary.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Verifier expects VSAs to be issued by TheRepository. Adversary
builds a malicious package and then issues a VSA of their own for the malicious
package. Solution: Verifier rejects because they only accept VSAs from
TheRepository which the adversary cannot issue since they do not have the
corresponding signing key.</p>
</details>
<details><summary>Upload package without provenance or VSA <span>(Build L1)</span></summary>
<p><em>Threat:</em> Replace the original package with a malicious one without provenance.</p>
<p><em>Mitigation:</em> Verifier requires provenance or a VSA before accepting the package.</p>
<p><em>Example:</em> Adversary replaces MyPackage with a malicious version of MyPackage
on the package repository and deletes existing provenance. Solution: Verifier
rejects because provenance is missing.</p>
